                                          A BRANCH SET STRATIFICATION FOR STATIONARY VARIFOLDS WITH
                                                             EPSILON-REGULARITY

                                                                 BRIAN KRUMMEL, PAUL MINTER, AND NESHAN WICKRAMASEKERA



                                                  Abstract. Suppose V is a class of stationary integral n-varifolds in B2n+k (0) ⊂ Rn+k which is
                                                  closed under weak limits, homotheties, rotations, and disjoint decomposition, and suppose that V
                                                  satisfies an ε-regularity property near planes of (integer) multiplicity ≤ Q ∈ {2, 3, . . . }. This last




arXiv:2606.01511v1 [math.DG] 1 Jun 2026
                                                  condition, more precisely, requires that there be a constant ϵ = ϵ(V, Q) ∈ (0, 1) such that if V ∈ V
                                                  is, in the unit cylinder Rk × B1n (0), ϵ-close as varifolds to the plane {0} × Rn taken with multiplicity
                                                  ≤ Q then, in the half-cylinder Rk × B1/2    n
                                                                                                 (0), V is represented by the graph of a Lipschitz multi-
                                                                           n
                                                  valued function over B1/2 (0) with uniform quantitative estimates of a C 1,α nature. For any varifold
                                                  in such a class V, we prove that the set of branch points with density ≤ Q has Hausdorff dimension
                                                  ≤ n − 2.
                                                      By choosing suitable V, a direct consequence of this result and the recently established regularity
                                                  theorems in [BKMW25, MW24] is that if V is a stationary integral n-varifold which is either: (a)
                                                  represented by the graph of a 2-valued Lipschitz function; or (b) codimension one, stable, and with
                                                  no classical singularities of density < Q, then the Hausdorff dimension of the density Q branch set
                                                  (Q = 2 in (a)) is at most n − 2.
                                                      The result in (a) together with the earlier work of Becker-Kahn [BK17] improves on the recent
                                                  work of Hirsch–Spolaor [HS24] (which proved the singular-set dimension upper bound n − 1 for
                                                  stationary 2-valued Lipschitz graphs) by providing a structural decomposition of the singular set
                                                  as the union of a piece contained within an (n − 1)-dimensional C 1,α submanifold and a piece that
                                                  is a closed set of Hausdorff dimension ≤ n − 2.
                                                      The varifold class in (b) includes as a special case area minimising hypersurfaces mod p; thus, our
                                                  result in this case together with [MW24] subsumes the recent work of De Lellis–Hirsch–Marchese–
                                                  Spolaor–Stuvard [DLHM+ 22] (while also providing the additional conclusion that the mod p min-
                                                  imising hypersurface near every branch point is a Lipschitz multi-valued graph with generalised-C 1,α
                                                  regularity).
                                                      Our proof utilises the planar frequency function introduced by the first and third authors in
                                                  their work on area minimising currents ([KW23a]), and thus does not require the Almgren center
                                                  manifold for the analysis of branch points except in a single, geometrically canonical case where
                                                  the center manifold satisfies additional simplifying properties. We prove that the planar frequency
                                                  is approximately monotone at density Q branch points for V ∈ V. We then partition the density Q
                                                  branch set into two pieces, namely those points where the planar frequency value is ̸= 2 and those
                                                  where it is exactly 2. The former we show can be stratified, directly using the planar frequency
                                                  function, by the spine dimension of tangent maps relative to the tangent plane at branch points.
                                                  For any point in the latter, we construct a single center manifold which touches V at that point
                                                  and at all nearby branch points of the same type, and stratify such branch points by the spine
                                                  dimension of the tangent maps arising from a linearisation procedure of V relative to this center
                                                  manifold.
                                                      For situations where energy convergence in the center-manifold linearisation procedure is not
                                                  readily available, our analysis uses an elegant alternative argument of Hirsch–Spolaor in [HS24]
                                                  that treats these tangent maps as multi-valued gradient Young measures.

                                                                       1. Introduction
Understanding the size of the branch set of general stationary integral n-varifolds is a question
lying at the heart of geometric measure theory. A well-known open question is whether the singular
set (or, equivalently, the branch set) can have positive Hn -measure.1 Even the existence question,
namely whether within a given closed Riemannian manifold (M n+k , g) one can always find a closed,
partially regular, n-dimensional minimal surface, is completely open except when k = 1 (or n = 1).
The Almgren–Pitts min-max theory provides the existence of a stationary integral n-varifold, but
extremely little is known about its regularity, precisely due to the possibility of a large branch set.
Indeed, essentially all that is known in this generality is that the (open) set of regular points is
a dense subset of the support of the varifold, which follows from the regularity theory of Allard
[All72].
Let us briefly elaborate on two situations where significant progress has been made. The first is
when the codimension is one and the varifold is additionally known to be stable; this is the situation
one has in the Almgren–Pitts min-max theory when k = 1. The work of Schoen–Simon [SS81] gives
a regularity and compactness theory for stable minimal hypersurfaces which are a priori known to
have sufficiently small singular sets (an assumption which, by virtue of the regularity theory for
area-minimising hypersurfaces, can be verified in the context of the Almgren–Pitts min-max the-
ory), showing that the singular set must in fact have Hausdorff dimension ≤ n − 7; see also the
recent breakthrough of Bellettini [Bel25] for extensions. Work of the third author [Wic14] pro-
vides a generalisation of the Schoen–Simon theory. This latter theory has been utilised to give an
alternative proof of the existence of minimal hypersurfaces in closed Riemannian manifolds; this
approach uses, in place of direct varifold min-max constructions, a much more elementary PDE
1We note that when the mean curvature is non-zero singular sets of positive Hn -measure can occur, even if the mean
curvature is arbitrarily close to 0 in L∞ .
             BRIAN KRUMMEL, PAUL MINTER, AND NESHAN WICKRAMASEKERA                                   3

theoretic min-max principle applied to the Allen–Cahn equation ([Gua18]). The energy concen-
tration set arising from these PDE min-max solutions is a stationary integral varifold ([HT00]) to
which the regularity theory of [Wic14] applies (see [TW12]). Most recently, the work [Wic14] has
been adapted in a similar phase-transition approach to the existence of optimally-regular, constant-
mean-curvature and prescribed-mean-curvature hypersurfaces in closed Riemannian manifolds (see
[BW18, BW19, BW20]). On the regularity side, building on the methods of [Wic14] and a key
estimate from [Bel25] (and aided also by an intermediate result of Hong–Li–Wang ([HLW24])), a
sharp regularity and compactness theory for stable immersed hypersurfaces whose singular (i.e.
non-immersed) points form an Hn−2 -null set has very recently been established by the second au-
thor and Xiao ([MX26]), giving the conclusion that the singular set has Hausdorff dimension at
most n − 7.
One of the key results in [SS81, Wic14] is showing that branch points do not occur under the given
assumptions, and it is through ruling out branch points entirely that one is able to deduce further
information on the size of the singular set. This is analogous to how in the classical regularity
theory of area minimising hypersurfaces, one first rules out branch points using a decomposition of
the current into boundaries of Caccioppoli sets (see [Sim83]), whose singular points, by a theorem
of De Giorgi ([DG61], see also [Sim83]), cannot have tangent cones which are planes. As such, the
branch set is empty in these situations.
The other situation where significant progress has been made on the regularity question is area-
minimising rectifiable currents of codimension ≥ 2. The Federer–Fleming compactness theorem
([FF60]) implies the existence of area (mass)-minimising integral currents (cycles) arising as rep-
resentatives of non-trivial integral homology classes of compact Riemannian manifolds. Similarly,
subject to appropriate conditions, this powerful compactness theorem also provides solutions to the
(oriented) Plateau problem, producing area-minimisers in their relative homology classes. When
the codimension is ≥ 2, these solutions can develop branch points, and it is precisely for this rea-
son that the regularity question in higher codimension is significantly more complicated than in
codimension 1.
Almgren’s monumental 1983 work [Alm00] (clarified later by De Lellis–Spadaro [DLS14, DLS16a,
DLS16b]) showed that the Hausdorff dimension of the (interior) singular set of such a minimiser T
is ≤ n − 2 where n is the dimension of T . This bound is optimal when the codimension is ≥ 2 (but
can be improved to ≤ n−7 in codimension 1 as discussed above). With regard to Almgren’s method
of proof, a major source of additional difficulties is a rather complicated iterative construction of
so-called center manifolds corresponding to each branch point. This aspect of the classical theory,
and the exceeding technical output needed for this procedure, seem unavoidable if the question of
the size of the singular set is prioritised and studied in isolation (as done in the classical theory).
Very recently, a new framework for this problem has been developed by the first and the third
author (see [KW23a, KW23b, KW26a, KW26b, KW26c]). In this approach, the focus is shifted to
the local structure of the current. In particular, the question of Hn−2 -a.e. uniqueness of tangent
cones is tied intimately to the question of the size of the singular set (and to subsequent structural
questions). By introducing planar frequency – a new intrinsic frequency function for the current
which is shown to be approximately monotone subject to a decay condition – this approach simul-
taneously resolves a number of questions without employing center manifolds; namely: the Hn−2
a.e. uniqueness of tangent cones; the dimension of the part of the branch set that is most technically
demanding (from the classical view point of using center manifolds); local structural decomposi-
tion of this part as a finite union of disjoint locally (n − 2)-rectifiable pieces; and a similar local
structural decomposition of the set of all non-branch point singularities. This is then followed by
the dimension bound for the full singular set (and other outcomes), for which a center manifold is
invoked only to handle a canonical case where this device becomes necessary and at the same time,
4                BRANCH SET STRATIFICATION FOR STATIONARY VARIFOLDS

importantly, satisfies key additional geometric properties. In addition to providing a considerable
simplification of Almgren’s argument, this limited use of center manifolds is in fact what paves the
way to a general local structural study of the current, giving a higher-dimensional generalization
of the two-dimensional theory of Chang, White, and Micallef–White ([Cha88, Whi83, MW95]).
The specific overall new results obtained, in addition to Hn−2 -a.e. uniqueness of tangent cones,
include: higher order asymptotics at Hn−2 -a.e. branch point; a local structural decomposition of
the singular set into a finite disjoint union of locally compact, locally (n − 2)-rectifiable sets; and
frequency conditions for a branch point to have a neighborhood in which the support of the current
is homeomorphic to an n-disk and admits a C 1,µ parameterisation – a conclusion directly anal-
ogous to the Micallef–White asymptotic description of a two-dimensional current near a branch
point where the current is irreducible. We mention for completeness that in contemporaneous and
independent work, De Lellis and Skorobogatova ([DLS25, DLS23]) have taken an approach based
on Almgren’s iterative center manifold constructions for all branch points, which together with
the work of the second author with De Lellis and Skorobogatova ([DLMS23]) provides two of the
above conclusions: Hn−2 -a.e. uniqueness of tangent cones and countable (n − 2)-rectifiability of the
singular set. These two approaches are conceptually and technically significantly different, except
for a certain excess-decay lemma in [DLMS23] and [KW23b] – based in part, in either case, on
[Wic14] – which is indispensable for both approaches.

As previously mentioned, when the ambient manifold has trivial homology of a certain degree one
can attempt to find a minimal surface of that dimension using instead the Almgren–Pitts min-
max theory, however this object is not area minimising and is just a stationary integral varifold
(conceivably with a form of Morse index control that could be exploited). Any further partial
regularity conclusion has, to date, been unobtainable precisely due to a lack of understanding of
the branch set of stationary varifolds.

In the present work, we investigate the size of the branch set of stationary integral n-varifolds
V in situations where one has an ε-regularity property near planes of multiplicity at most some
fixed Q ∈ {2, 3, . . . }. The ε-regularity property (see Definition 1.2) requires that for any integer
Q′ ∈ {2, 3, . . . , Q}, the structure of V locally about a density Q′ branch point is given by the graph
of a Lipschitz Q′ -valued function, which furthermore is generalised-C 1,α (see Definition 2.1). In
turn, this reduces the problem to understanding the size of the density Q′ branch set for Lipschitz
functions f : B1n (0) → AQ′ (Rk ) whose graphs are stationary for area, and furthermore satisfy
an ε-regularity property when ∥Df ∥L2 (B1n (0)) is sufficiently small, guaranteeing that f |B1/2 n (0) is

generalised-C 1,α with uniform, quantitative, estimates.

There are two situations which in recent years have been shown to satisfy such an ε-regularity
property for suitable Q. These are:

    (a) Q = 2 and V corresponds to the graph of a Lipschitz 2-valued function. Indeed, in recent
        work with S. Becker-Kahn [BKMW25], the second and third authors show that such V sat-
        isfy an ε-regularity theorem near multiplicity 2 planes. In fact, this conclusion holds under
        a significantly weaker hypothesis – a structural condition on the region {X : ΘV (X) < 2}
        – in place of the assumption that the entire varifold is a 2-valued Lipschitz graph; see
        [BKMW25, Definition 0.1].

    (b) Q is arbitrary, V has codimension one and is stable on any orientable portion of the regular
        part, and V contains no classical singularities of density < Q. In the work [MW24], the
        second and third authors establish that such V satisfy an ε-regularity theorem near multi-
        plicity Q planes. This situation contains as a special case varifolds associated to currents
        which are area minimising mod p hypersurfaces.
              BRIAN KRUMMEL, PAUL MINTER, AND NESHAN WICKRAMASEKERA                                         5

Remark 1.1. One might hope that in the future it is possible to prove such an ε-regularity
property in more general situations, such as when V corresponds to the graph of a Lipschitz Q-
valued function for arbitrary Q ∈ {2, 3, 4, . . . }. This is one of the reasons that the present paper is
phrased in this more general framework.

1.1. Main results. Throughout the paper we fix integers, n ≥ 2, k ≥ 1, and write P0 := {0}k ×Rn .

associated to graph(f ), i.e. the varifold push-forward of B1n (0) under f . We also write G for the
Almgren metric on the set AQ (Rk ) of unordered Q-points in Rk , namely

To state our main results we introduce the type of varifold class we consider. Let V denote a set
of stationary integral n-varifolds V in B2n+k (0) ⊂ Rn+k . We will assume that V is closed under
natural geometric operations and weak limits, namely:
      • For any V ∈ V, x0 ∈ B2n+k (0), ρ ∈ (0, 21 (2 − |x0 |)), and rotation Γ ∈ SO(n + k), we have
        (Γ ◦ ηx0 ,ρ )# V B2n+k (0) ∈ V.
      • If (Vj )j ⊂ V has lim supj→∞ ∥Vj ∥(B2n+k (0)) < ∞, then there is a subsequence (j ′ ) and
        V ∈ V (possibly V = 0) such that Vj ′ ⇀ V in B2n+k (0).
      • If V ∈ V is decomposable in the sense that V = V1 + V2 where V1 , V2 are integral n-varifolds
        with disjoint supports in B2n+k (0), then V1 , V2 ∈ V.
Here, ηx0 ,ρ (y) := (y − x0 )/ρ is the standard homothety Rn+k → Rn+k and ⇀ denotes convergence
as varifolds.
Fix an integer Q ∈ {2, 3, . . . }. The other assumption we make on V is that varifolds V ∈ V satisfy
an ε-regularity property near planes of multiplicity at most Q:

multiplicity Q if there exists ε = ε(V, Q) ∈ (0, 1) such that the following holds. If V ∈ V \ {0}

Remark 1.4. One would expect an ε-regularity theorem holding near a multiplicity Q plane to
imply that any ε-regularity theorem also holds at multiplicity Q′ < Q planes, and so the need in the
above to work with all multiplicities ≤ Q would reduce to working with just multiplicity Q. Indeed,
this is certainly the case when the class V is determined by structural or variational assumptions,
as if V ∈ V was close to a multiplicity Q′ < Q plane P , one could consider V + (Q − Q′ )(P + z) for
a small z ∈ P ⊥ (for which P + z and V are disjoint) and this varifold would also lie in V and now
be close a multiplicity Q plane. However, it is not entirely clear how to deduce this abstractly from
a single regularity result near multiplicity Q planes. To phrase our results appropriately (and since
one expects that in order to verify an ε-regularity theorem near multiplicity Q planes, one needs
to have already verified one near planes of multiplicity < Q) we work with Definition 1.2 as stated.

Remark 1.5. When Q = 2 in Definition 1.2, as soon as one has the conclusion that Ve is represented
by a Lipschitz 2-valued graph, all the subsequent conclusions regarding generalised-C 1,α regularity
and the corresponding estimates follow from [BKMW25]. This is the basis for our application to
the class of Lipschitz 2-valued stationary graphs. It is perhaps reasonable to suspect that the ε-
regularity theorem in [BKMW25] generalises to all Q ∈ {2, 3, 4, . . . }, at which point not only does
the work become applicable to Lipschitz Q-valued stationary graphs, but Definition 1.2 simplifies,
only requiring the conclusion of being represented by a Lipschitz multi-valued graph.

Our main result is then the following. Here, we write BV for the branch set of a stationary integral
varifold V , i.e. the set of singular points where at least one tangent cone is a supported on a plane
(which necessarily has integer multiplicity > 1 by Allard’s regularity theorem).

Theorem A. Let V be a class of varifolds which is closed under natural geometric operations and
weak limits (see the discussion preceding Definition 1.2). Suppose also that V obeys the ε-regularity
               BRIAN KRUMMEL, PAUL MINTER, AND NESHAN WICKRAMASEKERA                                       7

property up to multiplicity Q (see Definition 1.2). Then:

                                       dimH (BV ∩ {ΘV = Q}) ≤ n − 2.

In fact, by induction we have dimH (BV ∩ {ΘV ≤ Q}) ≤ n − 2, and so by standard stratification
results
                            dimH (sing(V ) ∩ {ΘV < Q + 1}) ≤ n − 1.

Remark 1.6. In fact, our proof of Theorem A provides a more precise description of the density
Q branch set by providing a certain stratification of it. Writing BVQ := BV ∩ {ΘV = Q}, we
will show that one can associate a (planar ) frequency value to each z ∈ BVQ , which is a number
NV (z) ∈ [1 + α, ∞), where α ∈ (0, 1) is as in Definition 1.2. We then have:
                                                           (1)         (1)
                                  BVQ ∩ {NV < 2} = S0 ∪ · · · ∪ Sn−2 ;

                                                           (2)         (2)
                                  BVQ ∩ {NV ≥ 2} = S0 ∪ · · · ∪ Sn−2 ;
                    (i)
where dimH (Sj ) ≤ j for each i ∈ {1, 2} and j ∈ {0, 1, . . . , n − 2}. When n = 2, we therefore have

                                                     (1)         (2)
                                              BVQ = S0 ∪ S0
                                 (2)                                                  (1)   (2)
and furthermore in this case S0 is a relatively closed subset of BVQ and both S0 , S0 are discrete.
This discreteness follows in an analogous manner to how for stratifications arising from a mono-
                                                                                  (1)   (2)
tonicity formula, the 0th -strata is discrete (as for us a limit point of either S0 or S0 would create a
non-collapsed 2-dimensional blow-up which has a 1-dimensional touching set, which is impossible).
Thus, when n = 2 the only possible branch points which are not isolated are those of planar fre-
quency ≥ 2 which are limit points of branch points of planar frequency < 2. In fact, for any ε > 0,
                                                           (2)          (2)
we can analogously stratify BVQ ∩ {NV ≥ 35 + ε} = S̃0 ∪ · · · ∪ S̃n−2 for arbitrary n, meaning that
when n = 2 the only situation in which we can have (distinct) density Q branch points (Zj )j ⊂ BVQ ,
Z ∈ BVQ with Zj → Z is when NV (Z) ≥ 2 and2 lim supj→∞ NV (Zj ) ≤ 35 , and so there must be a
genuine jump in the frequency value (in fact, one must even have NV (Z) ∈ {2, 3, 4, . . . }, else one
can get a contradiction by performing a suitable blow-up off the tangent plane at Z). Conjecturally
this should not happen when n = 2.

Theorem A is directly applicable in several situations. Indeed, consider the following two classes:

      • For L ∈ (0, ∞), write VL for the set of stationary integral n-varifolds in B2n+k (0) which are
        represented by the graph of a Lipschitz 2-valued function (or a single-valued function) over
        some n-dimensional subspace of Rn+k with Lipschitz constant ≤ L.

      • For Q ∈ {2, 3, . . . }, write SQ for the set of stationary integral n-varifolds in B2n+1 (0) which
        are stable on their regular part and have no classical singularities of density < Q.

These classes have been studied in [BKMW25, MW24], respectively, with ε-regularity theorems
established for both. As such, VL and SQ satisfy the assumptions of Theorem A (with Q = 2 in
the former case). We therefore have the following.


2See Remark 4.2 and Remark 4.14 for the explanation for why we get this improved upper bound on the frequency
value on the Zj .

Lipschitz function u : B1n (0) → A2 (Rk ) for which V = v(u). Then

                                                dimH (BV ) ≤ n − 2.

Consequently, dimH (sing(V )) ≤ n − 1. Moreover, when n = 2 we have
                                                           (1)     (2)
                                                 BV = S0 ∪ S0

Corollary 1.8. Let Q ∈ {2, 3, . . . } and V ∈ SQ . Then

                                        dimH (BV ∩ {ΘV = Q}) ≤ n − 2.

         (1)         (2)                                                                   (2)

subset of BV ∩ {ΘV < Q + 1}.

Remark 1.9. In Corollary 1.7, the claim on the full singular set follows directly from the branch
set dimension bound and general stratification theorems for singularities of stationary integral
varifolds. This part of Corollary 1.7 was recently established by Hirsch–Spolaor [HS24]. In the
special case of Corollary 1.7 where one knows that u is C 1,α rather than just Lipschitz, Corollary
1.7 was already known from the work of Simon and the third author [SW16]. The proof in [SW16]
uses purely PDE-theoretic methods (in particular without the need for an Almgren-type center
manifold construction, as, morally, the average of the two values is used as the center manifold)
and in fact subsequent work by the first and third authors [KW21] gives (again without employing
the Almgren center manifolds) that the branch set is countably (n − 2)-rectifiable, with unique
tangent maps at Hn−2 -a.e. branch point. Notice that the results in [SW16] are not otherwise
applicable in Corollary 1.7, since the ε-regularity theorem in [BKMW25] only provides generalised-
C 1,α regularity locally about branch points, not C 1,α regularity (although in codimension one it is
applicable, as then generalised-C 1,α regularity combined with stationarity implies C 1,α ).

Remark 1.10. For p ≥ 2 even, the class Sp/2 contains all varifolds associated to currents which are
area minimising hypersurfaces modulo p, and thus Corollary 1.8 includes as a special case the main
result established in [DLHM+ 22] (this is because all branch points have density p/2 in this case).
Since branch points in area minimising hypersurfaces mod p necessarily3 have planar frequency
≥ 2, it also shows that a two-dimensional surface in a smooth 3-manifold which is locally area

3This follows from the analysis in [DLHM+ 22]. It should be noted that this is a significant difference to the stable
hypersurface setting analysed in [MW24], as in the latter case it is possible for branch points to have planar frequency
< 2. The reason for this difference is precisely due to the fact that an area minimising hypersurface mod p must satisfy
a special orientation condition at classical singularities, namely that the unit conormals at the boundary must be
orientated in the same manner. This is a consequence of the minimising mod p assumption. Nonetheless, if one were
to assume such an orientation structure holds locally about a given branch point in a stable minimal hypersurface
in Sp/2 , one could similarly show that the planar frequency of the branch point must be ≥ 2. We refer the reader to
[MS26] for further discussion.
             BRIAN KRUMMEL, PAUL MINTER, AND NESHAN WICKRAMASEKERA                                    9

minimising mod p has a discrete branch set (cf. Remark 1.6). We therefore have (this has also
independently been recently established in [SSS25]):

Corollary 1.11. A 2-dimensional area minimising surface mod p in a closed smooth 3-dimensional
Riemannian manifold has isolated branch points.

In the two-valued Lipschitz case as in Corollary 1.7, the work of Hirsch–Spolaor [HS24] establishes
the Hausdorff dimension bound n − 1 (on the entire singular set) by showing that the classical
Almgren program in its full extent – namely, the construction of sequences of center manifolds
and intervals of flattening for arbitrary branch points – can be adapted to this setting. In this
argument, as in the classical theory, all branch points are treated equally, since the argument
proceeds without the knowledge of the uniqueness of tangent cones (and suitable quantitative
estimates). Consequently, the argument does not seem to readily provide a mechanism for dealing
with possible “frequency 1” branch points, meaning that it only yields the conclusion that the
branch set has Hausdorff dimension ≤ n − 1. (This is not as issue in the classical setting of are-
minimisers, and the optimal n − 2 conclusion follows, as the linearisation process produces Dirichlet
energy minimisers.)

By contrast, in the present work, we use the regularity theory of [BKMW25] to establish the optimal
result, namely that the branch set has Hausdorff dimension ≤ n−2. Though arguably the regularity
theory in [BKMW25] is a “heavy tool,” it leads to the optimal results and also to considerable
simplifications is other ways. It allows us to introduce, to the present not-necessarily-minimising
setting, the planar frequency function developed by the first and third authors in their work on area
minimising currents [KW23a]. The planar frequency function allows one to treat branch points with
planar frequency < 2 directly (and even those with planar frequency > 2), namely by performing
a blow-up procedure relative to the (unique) tangent plane; no center manifolds are needed. For
the branch points of planar frequency ≥ 2, we are able to use a significantly simplified version of
Almgren’s program, building just one center manifold, avoiding the need for both changing center
manifolds and intervals of flattening – which are technically demanding foundational ingredients in
the classical theory. In situations where energy convergence is not readily available in the blow-up
procedure relative to this center manifold (cf. Remark 1.16), we use an elegant alternative argument
from [HS24] which treats blow-ups as multi-valued gradient Young measures.

Remark 1.12. In situations where one can show energy convergence when blowing-up relative
to the center manifold (as is the case when the varifold is that associated to an area minimising
current, or a current which is area minimising mod p, using a competitor argument), the language
of multi-valued gradient Young measures is not needed, and one can work with multi-valued W 1,2
functions instead (indeed, the former then reduces to the latter).

Combining Corollary 1.7 with the regularity theories in [BK17, BKMW25] as well as simple tan-
gent cone analysis, we immediately deduce the following structural result for stationary 2-valued
Lipschitz graphs.

Theorem B. There exists α = α(n, k) ∈ (0, 1) such that the following is true. Suppose that
u : B1n (0) → A2 (Rk ) is a Lipschitz 2-valued function with the property that the varifold V = v(u)
is stationary. Write U ⊂ B1n (0) for the (open) subset of B1n (0) consisting of the points locally about
which u is generalised-C 1,α . Then we have

                                      dimH (B1n (0) \ U ) ≤ n − 3.
10                  BRANCH SET STRATIFICATION FOR STATIONARY VARIFOLDS

In fact, we have the disjoint union

                                        spt∥V ∥ = R ∪ B ∪ C ∪ K

and that U = B1n (0) \ π(K ), where π : Rk × Rn → Rn is the orthogonal projection map and:
     (i)      • R is the set of regular points of V ;
              • B is the set of singular points of V where at least one tangent cone is a plane of
                multiplicity 2 (this is then the unique tangent cone and locally about the point V has a
                generalised-C 1,α structure by [BKMW25, Theorem A]);
              • C is the set of singular points of V where at least one tangent cone consists of either a
                sum of 2 distinct planes or 4 distinct half-planes with a common boundary (this is then
                the unique tangent cone and locally about the point V has a generalised-C 1,α structure
                by [BK17]);
              • K is a closed subset which consists of the singular points of V where no tangent cone
                can be written as the sum of 4 half-planes (including the possibility of two planes which
                may or may not coincide).
     (ii)     • dimH (B) ≤ n − 2;
              • dimH (C ) ≤ n − 1 (in fact, C is locally contained within an (n − 1)-dimensional C 1,α
                submanifold by [BK17]);
              • dimH (K ) ≤ n − 3.
            In particular, as sing(V ) = B ∪ C ∪ K , we have dimH (sing(V )) ≤ n − 1.

Proof. All the claims except the Hausdorff dimension on K follow from Theorem A and the regular-
ity theories established in [BK17, BKMW25]. To establish the Hausdorff dimension on K , we will
analyse the possible tangent cones to show that K must be a subset of the usual (n − 3)-stratum,
and thus the claimed dimension bound follows.
Indeed, suppose x ∈ sing(V ) and let C be a tangent cone at x. If dim(S(C)) ∈ {n − 1, n},
then one may deduce that C is the unique tangent cone and V has a GC 1,α structure locally
about x by applying the regularity theorems in [BK17, BKMW25]. Thus, x ̸∈ K . Now suppose
dim(S(C)) = n − 2. We claim that C must be a sum of two planes: once we have shown this one
may apply [BK17] to see that V is smoothly immersed locally about x, and thus x ̸∈ K , which
completes the proof.
Since V is a two-valued Lipschitz graph it follows that C is also represented by the graph of a
Lipschitz 2-valued function f : Rn → A2 (Rk ). By quotienting out by the spine of C, we may in
fact assume that C is a 2-dimensional cone with S(C) = {0}, so that f : R2 → A2 (Rk ). By the
homogeneity of C and Theorem A, we know that the branch set of C is contained within {0}, and
thus the only possible singularities of C away from {0} must be classical singularities. Each classical
singularity determines a ray of classical singularities in the domain of f : let N ∈ {0, 1, 2, . . . } be
the number of such rays (we allow for N = 0 where there are no classical singularities). Note that
by a simple compactness argument on the link C ∩ S k+1 there must be only finitely many such
rays, so N < ∞. If N ≥ 1, denote the rays (through the origin) of the classical singularities by
ℓ1 , . . . , ℓN ⊂ R2 . Then R2 \ ∪N         N
                                  i=1 ℓi = ∪i=1 Wi , where in (suitable chosen) polar coordinates (r, θ) in
  2
R each Wi takes the form Wi = {r > 0, θi < θ < θi+1 } for some 0 = θ1 < · · · < θN +1 = 2π. On the
regions Wi × Rk , C is a 2-dimensional minimal cone which is the cone over two (smooth) geodesic
arcs in S k+1 , and thus C must be the sum of two planes here, which intersect only along θ = θi , θi+1 .
             BRIAN KRUMMEL, PAUL MINTER, AND NESHAN WICKRAMASEKERA                                 11

But two distinct planes can only intersect along a subspace, and thus we need θi + π = θi+1 . But
since 0 ≤ θi ≤ 2π for all i, this forces N = 2 in this situation (with θ1 = π), and therefore in
general we must have N ∈ {0, 2}. But then in the case N = 0, we have that C coincides with
the sum of two planes, and so we are done. The only remaining case is when N = 2, in which
case C is the sum of 4 half-planes. But then this would force ΘC (0) = 2, and since the classical
singularities also have density 2, we would need that the classical singularities belong to the spine,
and so dim(S(C)) = 1, again giving the contradiction. Thus we need C to be the sum of two planes
only intersecting along a subspace of dimension n − 2, completing the proof.                        □

Remark 1.13. It might be possible to improve the bound on the Hausdorff dimension of K in
Theorem B (and hence of B1n (0) \ U ) to ≤ n − 4, which would be the best possible dimension
bound in arbitrary codimension due to the examples constructed by Lawson–Osserman [LO77]. In
codimension one, in fact one has K = ∅ (and hence has U = B1n (0)), see [Hie20, Theorem 5.2].

Combining Corollary 1.8 with the regularity theories in [MW24, Min24], we immediately deduce
the following structural result for V ∈ SQ :

Theorem C. Suppose V ∈ SQ . Then,

                    spt∥V ∥ ∩ {ΘV < Q + 1} = R≤Q ∪ B Q ∪ C Q ∪ C˜Q+1/2 ∪ K ,

where
     • R≤Q is the set of regular points of V with density in {1, 2, . . . , Q}, and thus about which
       spt∥V ∥ is a locally smooth submanifold;
     • BQ ≡ BV ∩ {ΘV = Q} is the set of density Q flat singular points of V ; by [MW24],
       locally about each point in B Q we have that V is a Q-valued generalised-C 1,α graph, and by
       Corollary 1.8 we know B Q has Hausdorff dimension ≤ n − 2;
     • C Q is the set of density Q classical singularities of V , and locally about each such point V
       is a smooth classical singularity (see [Kru14]);
     • C˜Q+1/2 is the set of points where one tangent cone to V is a classical cone of density Q+1/2;
       by [Min24], it follows that such a tangent cone is unique, and V is locally about each such
       point a (possibly branched) generalised-C 1,α perturbation of its tangent cone;
     • K is a relatively closed subset of {ΘV < Q + 1} which has dimension ≤ n − 2; in fact, K
       equals the (n − 2)-strata of V in {ΘV < Q + 1}.
In particular, dimH (sing(V ) ∩ {ΘV < Q + 1}) ≤ n − 1. Here, α = α(n, Q) ∈ (0, 1).

1.2. Overview of the proof. We begin with some technical preliminaries which follow from the
ε-regularity property in Definition 1.2. In particular, we establish suitable doubling conditions for
the energy of the Lipschitz multi-valued graphs representing V ∈ V about a branch point.
The first key step in the proof is to use these preliminaries to prove approximate monotonicity
of the planar frequency function, as introduced in [KW23a]. Indeed, we will show that at every
density Q branch point (which by hypothesis has a unique tangent cone and, again by hypothesis,
about which the structure of the varifold is given by the graph of a generalised-C 1,α function as in
Definition 1.2), the planar frequency function is approximately monotone at all scales. Thus, we
may associate a planar frequency value at every such point, which we then show takes values in
[1 + α, ∞). In particular, the planar frequency value always has a fixed gap above 1, thus ruling
12                 BRANCH SET STRATIFICATION FOR STATIONARY VARIFOLDS

out “frequency 1” branch points; we stress that this is stronger than in the corresponding situation
for area minimisers (cf. [KW23a]).
We then partition the set of (density Q) branch points into two (disjoint) pieces:
      • B ̸=2 , those where the planar frequency is valued in [1 + α, ∞) \ {2};
      • B 2 , those where the planar frequency is equal to 2.
We then show (as observed in [KW26a]) that B ̸=2 has Hausdorff dimension ≤ n − 2 using only the
planar frequency function. Indeed, we will show that the coarse blow-ups arising from a sequence
of vertical rescalings of a given V ∈ V about a branch point are always homogeneous, with degree
of homogeneity equal to the planar frequency value at the chosen point. At this stage, we can
then argue the dimension bound for B ̸=2 using either of the two usual “dimension reduction”
arguments. We opt for that of Almgren [Alm00] as opposed to Federer [Fed70], namely we stratify
B ̸=2 by the spine dimension of the coarse blow-ups and show that nearby points in B ̸=2 of “good”
planar frequency value must accumulate around the spine of the coarse blow-up. Due to the planar
frequency value lying in (1, ∞), the spine of these coarse blow-ups must be a subspace of dimension
≤ n − 2; we stress that having a frequency gap above 1 is what ensures that this spine dimension is
not n − 1. The desired dimension bound for this subset of the branch set then follows by standard
measure-theoretic arguments.
Let us elaborate on this last point that nearby points in B ̸=2 of “good” planar frequency value
must accumulate around the spine of the coarse blow-up, as it will illustrate why branch points of
planar frequency exactly 2 must be treated differently. The planar frequency function at a given
branch point measures the frequency of V relative to the tangent plane to V at the point (which
is unique, by Definition 1.2). However, the tangent plane may differ from point-to-point, and thus
branch points with different tangent planes have different “frequency functions”. The spine of the
coarse blow-up is however determined by the points of maximum frequency for a specific choice
of plane, and so it is not a priori clear that different branch points should accumulate along the
spine. However, we will show (similarly to [KW26a]), for B ̸=2 , that when we perform a coarse
blow-up, all these potentially different tangent planes at the relevant points in B ̸=2 coincide in the
coarse blow-up. As such, in the coarse blow-up the frequency functions at the limit points of these
branch points are measuring the frequency of the coarse blow-up with respect to the same plane,
and this is what forces the accumulation of these points around the spine of the coarse blow-up.
The reason this argument fails for points where the planar frequency is exactly 2 is simply because
one cannot in general guarantee that the (possibly different) tangent planes at different points of
planar frequency 2 coincide in the coarse blow-up limit4.
To show that the set B 2 has Hausdorff dimension ≤ n−2, we construct a center manifold analogously
to Almgren [Alm00] (see also [DLS16a]). This is needed, for instance, when the coarse blow-ups
collapse to a single (harmonic, homogeneous of degree 2) function with multiplicity Q. In fact, this
argument will also show that the set of branch points with planar frequency ≥ 2 has Hausdorff
dimension ≤ n − 2, although at this stage of the argument we only need to show this for the part
of the branch set where the planar frequency is exactly 2.
The reader who is familiar with Almgren’s center manifold construction will then note the benefit
of this approach: if one only wishes to study branch points where the decay to the (unique) tangent
4The reader may find it useful to think of this difference from the viewpoint of a Taylor expansion: measuring the
frequency with respect to the tangent plane is morally subtracting off the linear part of the Taylor expansion, leading
to the first possibly non-zero term being the quadratic term. Planar frequency 2 means this part does not vanish,
which is not a particularly stringent condition. Planar frequency > 2 forces this quadratic term to vanish, which is
more restrictive for, say, a harmonic function, and this is what allows us to show that the different tangent planes
coincide in this case.
              BRIAN KRUMMEL, PAUL MINTER, AND NESHAN WICKRAMASEKERA                                          13

plane is at least quadratic, the center manifold is significantly simpler to use, as one may avoid
the need for a sequence of changing center manifolds and intervals of flattening. Consequently, one
only needs to build a single center manifold, which furthermore will touch the varifold at all nearby
branch points of the same density where the decay to the tangent plane is at least quadratic. (This
fact follows directly from the construction and was likely known to Almgren in the 1980’s, and is
noted in the work of S. Chang [Cha88].) As such, we only need this simpler construction. This
is one of the significant advantages of using the planar frequency function, as already observed in
[KW23a], namely by avoiding the need to use a center manifold at branch points with subquadratic
decay to the tangent plane (or with non-unique tangent cone); this is the situation requiring an
infinite sequence of center manifolds in the classical theory. Consequently, we will give an essentially
self-contained construction of the center manifold, including the proof of the almost monotonicity
of the frequency function relative to the center manifold, with certain refinements. A significant
portion of this will follow the relevant parts of the works [DLS16a, DLS16b], which in turn are a
technically streamlined version of Almgren’s original work.

Remark 1.14. One might wonder, given the Lipschitz multi-valued graph structure about our
branch points, whether it is possible to use a simpler approach than Almgren’s center manifold to
establish the dimension bound for B 2 . For instance, one might wonder if it is possible to perform a
blow-up relative to the average of the multi-valued graph instead of the center manifold. The issue
with this at the moment appears to be a lack of regularity for the average, since if one wishes to
show monotonicity of a frequency quantity relative to some submanifold, that submanifold needs to
be C 3 due to error terms involving derivatives of the mean curvature of the submanifold appearing
in the first variation computations (which arise from derivatives of the exponential map). Notice
that the center manifold is C 3,β , whilst we do not even know if the average-part of a stationary
Lipschitz multi-valued graph is C 2 in general5. Simply mollifying the average does not help either,
as one (ideally) wants the object to touch V at the desired branch points. If however one was in
a situation where it was known that the average did have this improved regularity, then it seems
reasonable that one could use the average as a replacement for the center manifold (for instance, if
the average-part was identically zero, this does work and indeed it reduces to the planar frequency).

Remark 1.15. One could alternatively attempt a method which falls between the planar fre-
quency and the center manifold, namely by trying to use a modification of the planar frequency
via measuring the frequency of V at a given branch point relative to some choice of, say, minimal
surface touching V at the branch point, and allowing the surface to vary from point-to-point. For
planar frequency, this choice of (minimal) surface is the tangent plane. However, it is unclear how
one might choose such surfaces in a manner which ensures both: (a) there is an approximately
monotone notion of frequency; and (b) the possibly different choices of surface at nearby points
collapse to a fixed one in the coarse blow-up limit (as was the case with planar frequency at points
of planar frequency ̸= 2); this latter condition is needed to know that in the blow-up the frequency
functions are the same. We stress that, for branch points of planar frequency ≥ 2, both of these
points are satisfied in the present work by taking the surface at different points to be a fixed one,
namely the single center manifold.

To aid the reader, we briefly mention that using a center manifold to control the dimension of
the branch points with planar frequency ≥ 2 is morally an argument using quantitative unique
continuation. Indeed, one can control the size of the touching set between two smooth minimal
surfaces using a frequency function. The geometric viewpoint of this is that one measures the

5It should be noted in the case of 2-valued C 1,α stationary graphs the work [SW16] provides a way around this
problem, utilising PDE-theoretic techniques to show that one can directly work with the average-free part of the
function.
14               BRANCH SET STRATIFICATION FOR STATIONARY VARIFOLDS

frequency of one surface relative to the other, and stratifies the touching set by looking at the
tangent maps, using elliptic estimates to linearise the problem (which in the setting of two minimal
graphs gives rise to homogeneous harmonic functions). When controlling the size of the branch
set of a given varifold, morally we wish to control the set of singular points where the surface is
touching itself (with “genuine” branch points being those where the “sheets” of the varifold are
indistinguishable, and so one cannot reduce the problem to the touching set of two minimal graphs).
The idea then is that, if one can find another (appropriate6) surface which touches the varifold along
some subset of the branch set, then one can control the size of that subset by controlling this part
of the touching set of two geometric objects as before. This is what the center manifold precisely
achieves, for the subset of the branch points where the planar frequency is ≥ 2. With this geometric
viewpoint, the argument is therefore a natural extension of typical quantitative unique continuation
arguments.
We also stress that, with this viewpoint, the main property one needs regarding the linearisation
procedure is that, when V is close to the manifold one is measuring frequency relative to (namely
the center manifold, or the tangent plane for planar frequency), it is close strongly in W 1,2 to
the linearised problem (after appropriate rescaling). This enables one to pass certain first variation
identities from V to the linearised problem (for example, to establish monotonicity of the frequency
function at the linearised level), which is all that is needed to conclude the argument. Other than
this, the precise form of the linearised problem is essentially irrelevant.

Remark 1.16. In more general situations, as in the present paper, this last point regarding strong
convergence in W 1,2 to the linearised problem (either relative to a plane or the center manifold)
is a non-trivial fact. In Appendix A we will see that under Definition 1.2 (as thus in the settings
                                                          1,2
of Corollary 1.7 and Corollary 1.8) we do have strong Wloc    (B1n (0)) convergence when linearising
relative to a plane (this uses the energy non-concentration estimate in [BKMW25, (B6)]). This
will be of great importance in our arguments, namely in establishing approximate monotonicity of
the planar frequency function.
For the linearisation procedure relative to the center manifold, in certain situations strong conver-
            1,2
gence in Wloc   can be directly verified (see Remark 1.12); for instance, in Almgren’s work on area
minimisers this is verified using a competitor argument.) In the present work, we use an argument
of Hirsch–Spolaor [HS24] which allows us to sidestep this issue by constructing blow-ups as multi-
valued gradient Young measures. These measures then form our blow-up class relative to the center
manifold, and we can similarly show that one may stratify branch points of planar frequency ≥ 2
by the spine dimension of these measures. This allows us to conclude the dimension bound again
by standard arguments.

Remark 1.17. As a final comment, we note that our approach is rather different to that used
by the third author and Simon in [SW16] when studying 2-valued C 1,α stationary graphs. As
mentioned, in [SW16] it was possible to avoid the use of Almgren center manifolds completely,
using a frequency function for the average-free part of the graph directly. Morally, if one can
‘subtract off’ the average and maintain all other analytic properties, such as an approximately
monotone frequency function and energy convergence to the linearised problem, then the above
geometric discussion is trivial, as one can simply look at the touching set between the average-free
part and a single plane (morally, the problem is then reduced to planar frequency). The reason why
this simplification was possible in [SW16] was that they were able to show that in fact the 2-valued
function was C 1,1/2 -regular, meaning that the coefficients of the corresponding (multi-valued) PDE
for the average-free part of the function are Lipschitz. This allows one to directly show that a
6The surface cannot be chosen completely arbitrarily due to the need for an approximately monotone frequency
function.
              BRIAN KRUMMEL, PAUL MINTER, AND NESHAN WICKRAMASEKERA                                       15

suitable variant of Almgren’s frequency function, now for the average-free part, is approximately
monotone using ideas from the work of Garofalo–Lin [GL86, GL87]. For us, we do not expect in
general to have C 1,1/2 regularity of our multi-valued graphs and so we seemingly cannot argue like
this. Our present argument in particular avoids looking at multi-valued PDEs.

Organisation of paper: In Section 2 we give some preliminaries and results concerning the ε-
regularity property. In Section 3 we introduce the planar frequency function, show its approximate
monotonicity at all density Q branch points, and then use it to establish the dimension bound on
the set of branch points where the planar frequency is ̸= 2. In Section 4, we then construct a
single center manifold and use it to show that the set of branch points where the planar frequency
is exactly 2 (in fact, is ≥ 2) also has Hausdorff dimension ≤ n − 2, thus completing the proof of
Theorem A. In Appendix A we discuss the problem of energy convergence for coarse blow-ups,
and in Appendix B we develop the theory of multi-valued gradient Young measures introduced by
Hirsch–Spolaor [HS24].

search Fellow. The authors would like to thank Sidney Stanbury for reading and providing valuable
feedback on a preliminary version of this paper.


                                            2. Preliminaries
Throughout we fix Q ∈ {2, 3, . . . } and a class V of stationary integral n-varifolds in B2n+k (0) which
satisfies:
      • V is closed under natural geometric operations and weak limits;
      • V obeys the ε-regularity property up to multiplicity Q (Definition 1.2).
In the present section, we will deduce consequences of the ε-regularity property holding up to
multiplicity Q. We refer the reader to [BKMW25, MW24] for further explanations of terminology
where needed. For simplicity, when constants depend on both V and Q, we simply write that they
depend on V.
For V ∈ V and P an n-dimensional subspace of Rn+k , we define the (one-sided ) L2 -height excess
of V relative to P by           Z

                                         πP  (P ∩B1n+k (0))

where πP : Rn+k → P is the orthogonal projection onto P . We abbreviate ÊV,P0 by ÊV , where we
recall P0 := {0}k × Rn . We also define the L2 -tilt excess of V relative to P by:
                                     Z

Notice that this is a very crude Hölder-type assumption: we are just requiring some uniform Hölder
continuity of the tangent cones on the Q-coincidence set, and separately local α-Hölder regularity
of the tangent cones away from the Q-coincidence set. This is therefore less restrictive than the
definitions given in the special cases seen in [BKMW25, MW24]; however it suffices for our purposes.

Now we recall the (multiplicity Q) coarse blow-up class associated to V, denoted BQ  V , which consists
of all coarse blow-ups v ∈ W 1,2 (B1n (0); AQ (Rk )) generated by V. These are functions generated by
performing a vertical (inhomogeneous) blow-up procedure of the sequence of multi-valued Lipschitz
approximations corresponding to a sequence (Vj )j ⊂ V which converge, as varifolds in Rk × B1n (0),
to the plane P0 with multiplicity Q. The reader can consult [BKMW25, Part 2] for the general
definition of a coarse blow-up, with a “coarse blow-up generated by V” being one in which the
sequence of varifolds all belong to V.

Remark 2.3. Under the assumptions of Theorem 2.2, if one additionally assumes that ΘV (0) ≥ Q
then one can directly verify the Poincaré-style inequality
                  Z                                    Z

for any given σ ∈ (0, 1), providing ε is small depending on σ as well. This follows from the
first variation formula and Almgren’s (weak) Lipschitz approximation with error terms controlled
by the tilt excess (arguing analogously to that seen in [Wic14, (7.4)]). Thus in this situation,
after rescaling, one can assume that the height excess and tilt excess on different cylinders are
comparable.

We now recall some of the main properties concerning coarse blow-ups v ∈ BV . These are direct
consequences of the strong convergence in W 1,2 to the coarse blow-up guaranteed in Definition 1.2
(cf. Appendix A) and the stationarity of the varifolds involved.

Theorem 2.4. If v ∈ BV has v ̸≡ 0 in B1n (0), then for any y ∈ B1n (0) and ρ ∈ (0, 1 − |y|) we have
that v ̸≡ 0 on any ball Bρn (y) ⊂ B1n (0), and that the frequency function

Nv (y) := limρ↓0 Nv,y (ρ) ∈ [0, ∞) exists for each y ∈ B1n (0) and is an upper semi-continuous function
of y.
Furthermore, all of these conclusions also hold for vf , the average-free part of v, in place of v.

                                      3. Planar Frequency
In this section we introduce the planar frequency function, originally used by the first and third
authors in their work on analysing the branch set of an area minimising current [KW23a]. In
this section we show its approximate monotonicity at density Q branch points of varifolds V ∈ V,
and then subsequently use it to show that the set of (density Q) branch points where the planar
frequency value is not equal to 2 has Hausdorff dimension ≤ n − 2.

Throughout, we fix V ∈ V and suppose Z ∈ spt∥V ∥ is such that V has one tangent cone at Z
being a plane P with multiplicity Q, and furthermore there is no δ > 0 for which V Bδn+k (Z) =
Q|P + Z| Bδn+k (Z) (e.g. Z ∈ sing(V )). Such assumptions hold, for instance, about a density Q
branch point of V . By Definition 1.2, we know that this tangent cone is unique and, by translating
and rotating, we may without loss of generality assume that Z = 0 and P = P0 ≡ {0}k × Rn .
Moreover, we know that there is some scale ρ > 0 for which the conclusions of Definition 1.2
apply to V in Bρ (0); by considering instead (η0,ρ )# V in place of V , we may without loss of
generality assume that ρ = 1. In particular, we may assume that there is a generalised-C 1,α function
      n (0) → A (Rk ) with u(0) = QJ0K, Du(0) = QJ0K, which further obeys the estimates and
u : B1/2         Q
conclusions in Definition 1.2 and Theorem 2.2 (recall also Remark 2.3, which gives comparability of
the height and tilt excess under the present assumptions; we also replace V with Ve as in Theorem
2.2). Thus, from the decay rate of the tilt excess as well as the Lipschitz bound, we have the
pointwise bound

(3.1)                       |Du(x)| ≤ C ÊV rα                              n

3.1. Approximate monotonicity of the planar frequency function. Let φ : [0, ∞) → R be
the Lipschitz function defined by
                                        
                                        1
                                               if s ∈ [0, 1/2);
                                 φ(s) := 2 − 2s if s ∈ [1/2, 1);
                                        
                                         0      if s ∈ [1, ∞).
                                        

Technically, in our arguments we will need to take a smooth approximation of φ and then take a
limit (cf. [KW23a, Proof of Lemma 4.5]). For ease of presentation, we shall not comment further
on this detail.
For ρ ∈ (0, 1/2) we define:
                                       Z
                                                             1
                      H(ρ) := −ρ1−n     dist2 (X, P0 )|∇S r|2 φ′ (r/ρ) dV (X, S),
                                                             r
                                          Z
                                    1 2−n
                           D(ρ) := ρ         ∥πS − πP0 ∥2 φ(r/ρ) dV (X, S).
                                    2
Here, r = |x| and X = (y, x) ∈ Rk × Rn ∼    = Rn+k , so that dist(X, P0 ) ≡ |y|. These are the same
intrinsic expressions as defined in [KW23a]. We then define the planar frequency function by

                                                       D(ρ)
                                            N (ρ) :=        .
                                                       H(ρ)

Note that this is well-defined. Indeed, if it were not then necessarily H(ρ) = 0, i.e. |u| ≡ 0 on
Bρ (0) \ B ρ/2 (0). But now take ζ ∈ Cc1 (B1n (0); R) with ζ ≡ 1 on B3ρ/4 (0) and ζ ≡ 0 outside
Bρ (0). Consider its vertical extension ζ̃(y, x) := ζ(x) for (y, x) ∈ Rk × B1n (0), and take any ζ̄ ∈
Cc1 (Rk × B1n (0)) which agrees with ζ̃ on a neighbourhood of spt∥V ∥. Then, for any κ ∈ {1, . . . , k},
by taking the function xκ ζ̄eκ in the first variation formula for V , where eκ is the unit vector in the
xκ -direction, we get         Z                         Z
                                 ζ̄|∇V xκ |2 d∥V ∥ = − xκ ∇V xκ · ∇V ζ̄.

By choice of ζ, the integrand on the right-hand side is supported on Rk ×(Bρ (0)\B 3ρ/4 (0))∩spt∥V ∥,
which is where we know xκ ≡ 0 (as |u| ≡ 0 here). Thus, we have for each κ ∈ {1, . . . , k},
                                        Z
                                           ζ̄|∇V xκ |2 d∥V ∥ = 0

which in turn implies that |Du| ≡ 0 on B3ρ/4 (0), i.e. u is constant in B3ρ/4 (0). But as |u| ≡ 0 on
B3ρ/4 (0) \ B ρ/2 (0), this gives that |u| ≡ 0 on Bρ (0). But then this means that V (Rk × Bρn (0)) ≡
Q|Bρn (0)|, giving a contradiction. Thus, N (ρ) is well-defined for each ρ ∈ (0, 1/2).
Notation: We write HV,P,Z (ρ), DV,P,Z (ρ), and NV,P,Z (ρ) for the above (intrinsic) quantities centred
at a point Z and relative to a plane P passing through Z. These are exactly the above expressions
24              BRANCH SET STRATIFICATION FOR STATIONARY VARIFOLDS

when applied to the varifold (Γ ◦ τZ )# V , where τZ (X) := X − Z and Γ is any rotation of Rn+k
sending P − Z to P0 .

Remark 3.1. When the codimension is 1, the above expressions for H(ρ) and D(ρ) take a much
simpler looking form in terms of the function u. This is due to the normal space to the graph of u
being 1-dimensional. Indeed, by direct computation (similar to those done below) one may check
that when k = 1:

Here, |Dθ u|2 = |Du|2 − |Dr u|2 is a shorthand notation, where Dr u := |x| · Du. Here, we are also
implicitly summing over the Q-values of u meaning, for example, that technically we have
                                    Q           Z

This makes very explicit the comparison between the intrinsic expressions for H(ρ) and D(ρ)
to those defined for a Dirichlet-minimising function. Here, we have additional factors coming
from the non-linear nature of the minimal surface equation. These additional factors (and their
counterparts in higher codimension) effectively allow us to deal with error terms which appear in
the first variation formula when establishing the approximate monotonicity of the planar frequency
function. Notice that each of these additional factors is comparable to 1, since

We now show that the planar frequency function is approximately monotone. We begin by com-

provided in (3.1) (this allows us to simplify the arguments needed to control certain error terms
which arise).

Recalling from (3.1) that |Du(x)| ≤ Lrα for a.e. x, we therefore have D(ρ) ≤ CL2 ρ2+2α , and so we

where C = C(V) and γ = γ(V) ≡ n+1+2α . This is the desired almost monotonicity of the planar
frequency.
To summarise, we have shown the following:

Theorem 3.2 (Approximate Monotonicity of Planar Frequency). There exists ε = ε(V) ∈ (0, 1)
such that the following holds. Suppose V ∈ V is such that 0 ∈ sing(V ) is a density Q singular point
of V with (unique) tangent cone Q|P0 | and ÊV < ε. Then, for all 0 < σ ≤ ρ < 1/4,
                     2      2γ(1+α) +C Ê2−2γ D(σ)γ                 2    2γ(1+α) +C Ê2−2γ D(ρ)γ
                 eC ÊV σ                V            N (σ) ≤ eC ÊV ρ                V            N (ρ),

and hence
                                                                    2   2γ(1+α)
                                          N (σ) ≤ N (ρ) · eC ÊV ρ                .
                                                                   2α
Here, α = α(V) ∈ (0, 1) is the constant from Definition 1.2, γ = n+1+2α , and C = C(V) ∈ (0, ∞).

Remark 3.3. In fact, now that one has a monotone frequency function, one can show a simpler
version of the doubling property one needed to control error∗2 . Consequently, one can then prove

                                         |error2 | + |error3 |
                                                               ≤ CL2 ρ2α−1
                                                D(ρ)

and consequently that
                                           d
                                              log N (ρ) ≥ −CL2 ρ2α−1 .
                                           dρ
This then gives the simpler-looking monotonicity property: for all 0 < σ ≤ ρ < 1/4,
                                             2   2α                 2   2α
                                         eC ÊV σ N (σ) ≤ eC ÊV ρ N (ρ).

This then simplifies the form of some properties later as well. We will not use this however, and
stick with the version in Theorem 3.2. We stress that the constant C in this remark would also
depend on the frequency value at (say) scale 1, whilst the corresponding constant C in Theorem
3.2 does not.
32                     BRANCH SET STRATIFICATION FOR STATIONARY VARIFOLDS

In the setting of Theorem 3.2, we see that the limit

                                                 NV (0) := lim N (ρ)
                                                             ρ↓0

always exists; we call NV (0) the planar frequency of V at 0. Of course, at any singular point of
such a varifold V where the (unique) tangent cone is a plane of multiplicity Q, then up to rotation,
translation, and scaling, the hypotheses of Theorem 3.2 are satisfied, and thus at any such singular
point Z we can define the frequency NV (Z) as the limit at 0 of the planar frequency centred at the
point Z and rotated so that the tangent plane is Q|P0 |.

Note: In the area-minimising case as in [KW23a], it is not known a priori whether there is a decay
rate above 1 at branch points, and the planar frequency function (among other things) is used to
establish that at Hn−2 a.e. branch point the decay rate must exceed 1 + α for some fixed constant
α depending only on n, m and a mass bound. See [KW23a], [KW23b]. In contrast to this, here
the generalised-C 1,α regularity of u guarantees (as just shown) that the planar frequency exists
and is approximately monotone at every such singular point, and the planar frequency takes values
≥ 1 + α.

Next, we deduce some elementary consequences of such a monotonicity result. The first is control
on the scale-invariant L2 -height in terms of the planar frequency function, which tells us that the
planar frequency captures the decay rate of the varifold to its tangent plane. This also provides a
uniform lower bound on the planar frequency value NV (0), which will be important for our blow-up
analysis later.

We also have upper semi-continuity of the planar frequency among singular points where the
(unique) tangent cone is a plane with multiplicity Q.

Corollary 3.5 (Upper Semi-Continuity of Planar Frequency). Suppose (Vj )j ⊂ V and V ∈ V are
such that Vj ⇀ V . Suppose also that Z ∈ sing(V ) is such that V has a (unique) tangent cone P
at Z which is a multiplicity Q tangent plane, and Zj ∈ spt∥Vj ∥ are such that Vj has a (unique)
tangent cone Pj at Zj which is a multiplicity Q tangent plane. Then:

                                         NV (Z) ≥ lim sup NVj (Zj ).
                                                          j→∞

In fact, for any ρj → ρ with ρ > 0 sufficiently small we have

                                       lim NVj ,Pj ,Zj (ρj ) = NV,P,Z (ρ).
                                       j→∞


Proof. We may translate to assume without loss of generality that Z = 0, and rescale to assume
(by Definition 1.2) that V is represented by a Lipschitz Q-valued graph in B1n+k (0). We may also
rotate to assume that the (unique) tangent cone to V at Z is Q|P0 |. Assuming j is sufficiently large,
we may assume that for all j we have ÊVj < ε, for ε as in Theorem 2.2. We may also translate
and rotate the Vj to assume that Zj = 0 and that the unique tangent cone to Vj at Zj is Pj := P0
for all j (this is possible as such operations do not change the value of the frequency function).
Thus, Vj , V (respectively) are all expressible in Rk × B1/2

(respectively) B1/2 (0) → AQ (R ), obeying the estimates in Theorem 2.2; note that these estimates

                   HVj ,Pj ,Zj (ρ) → HV,P,Z (ρ)          and        DVj ,Pj ,Zj (ρ) → DV,P,Z (ρ)

for all ρ ∈ (0, 1/4). Thus, as HV,P,Z (ρ) > 0 for all ρ ∈ (0, 1/4) (if not, then as shown before V must
agree with 2|P0 | in some neighbourhood of Z and so as mentioned the conclusion is automatically
true), we have
                          NVj ,Pj ,Zj (ρ) → NV,P,Z (ρ)    for any ρ ∈ (0, 1/4).
