Remark: The expression for the Almgren frequency function of a coarse blow-up v ∈ BV appearing
in Corollary 3.6 is slightly different than the one used in Theorem 2.4, since in Corollary 3.6 it
includes the cut-off function in the definition. If one takes a more general φ and lets φ → 1[0,1] ,
one can recover the version of the frequency function used in Theorem 2.4. The difference is not
substantial, however, and in Theorem 2.4 one could alternatively used the modified version above.
We stress however that the difference is more substantial for the planar frequency function, as
indeed our proof of the estimate controlling error∗2 depended on the gradient of the cut-off φ, and
the constant involved goes to ∞ as φ → 1[0,1] .

3.2. Dimension bound for branch points with planar frequency ̸= 2. Here we argue simi-
larly to the corresponding part in [KW26a]. Fix V ∈ V and write BVQ for the set of singular points
Z of V at which V has a (unique) tangent cone which is a plane with multiplicity Q. We know from
Theorem 3.2 that each Z ∈ BVQ has a well-defined planar frequency value which, from Corollary
3.4(iii), takes values in [1 + α, ∞) for some fixed α = α(V) ∈ (0, 1). We then partition BVQ into
               BRIAN KRUMMEL, PAUL MINTER, AND NESHAN WICKRAMASEKERA                                                   37

three subsets based on the planar frequency value as follows:

                                               BVQ = BV<2 ∪ BV2 ∪ BV>2

where for p ∈ [1, ∞), BV<p = {Z ∈ BVQ : NV (Z) < p}, BVp = {Z ∈ BVQ : NV (Z) = p}, and
BV>p = {Z ∈ BVQ : NV (Z) > p} (we have dropped the Q dependence in the notation for these latter
sets for simplicity).
In this section, we use the properties of the planar frequency previously established to prove a
dimension bound on the size of BV<2 and BV>2 . In Section 4 we will show the same dimension bound
for BV2 (and in fact, the argument in Section 4 also establishes the dimension bound for BV>2 ).
Our main result here is therefore:

Proposition 3.7. If V ∈ V, then dimH (BV<2 ∪ BV>2 ) ≤ n − 2.

Remark: Our argument will utilise Almgren’s dimension reduction via stratification [Alm00]. One
could also use Federer’s dimension reduction, arguing by contradiction assuming that Hs (B1 (0) ∩
(BV<2 ∪ BV>2 )) > 0 for some s > n − 2 and then performing a coarse blow-up about a good density
point for this set, using the approximate monotonicity of the planar frequency function to ultimately
reach a contradiction by producing a coarse blow-up which has a branch set of Hausdorff dimension
> n − 2. The advantage of arguing via Almgren’s stratification is that it will detect a subspace
which nearby points of “good frequency” must accumulate to, providing the natural starting point
towards proving that the branch set is countably (n − 2)-rectifiable (although we do not pursue
this question in the present work).

Proof. First, fix Z ∈ BVQ . Up to rotation and translation, without loss of generality we may assume
that Z = 0 and that the (unique) tangent cone to V at Z is Q|P0 |. Take any sequence σj ↓ 0, and
produce a coarse blow-up v ∈ BV of the sequence Vj := (η0,σj )# V . Corollary 3.6, with Pj ≡ P0
and Zj ≡ 0 for all j, gives that for each ρ > 0,

                                         NVj (ρ) ≡ NVj ,P0 ,0 (ρ) → Nv,0 (ρ)

(note that all of these quantities are well-defined by the estimates in Corollary 3.4). However, as the
Vj are simply rescalings of V about a fixed point, we know that NVj (ρ) = NVj ,P0 ,Z (ρρj ) → NV (Z)
for every ρ > 0, and thus we see that

                                                  Nv,0 (ρ) = NV (Z)

for every ρ > 0. In particular, this tells us that Nv (0) = NV (Z). As Nv,0 (ρ) is constant in ρ,
we have that v is a homogeneous function11 with degree of homogeneity exactly NV (Z) (here, as
explained in the footnote, we can ensure that v is naturally defined on all of Rn ). In particular,
v ̸≡ 0, since ∥vj ∥L2 (B1 (0) ≡ 1 and as vj → v uniformly on compact subsets of Rn we therefore
have ∥v∥L2 (B1 (0)) = 1. Moreover, by standard properties of frequency functions for homogeneous
blow-ups, we know that Nv (z) ≤ Nv (0) for all z ∈ Rn , and that if S(v) denotes the spine of v
11The reader should note that the estimates in Definition 1.2 and Arzelà–Ascoli readily give that the blow-up is
β-Hölder continuous for any β ∈ (0, 1). In fact, by Definition 1.2 and Corollary 3.4, for each ρ ∈ [1, ∞) we have
Vj Cρ (0) = v(uj ) where ∥uj ∥C 0,1 (Bρ (0)) ≤ Cρ ÊVj , provided j is sufficiently large, where Cρ ∈ (0, ∞) is a constant
depending on ρ but not on j. Thus, Arzelà–Ascoli gives that vj := uj /ÊVj → v uniformly on compact subsets of
Rn . Since vj → v locally uniformly, we also know that Lip(v|Bρ (0) ) ≤ lim inf j→∞ Lip(vj |Bρ (0) ) ≤ Cρ . Thus, we may
assume that v is defined on all of Rn .
38                BRANCH SET STRATIFICATION FOR STATIONARY VARIFOLDS

(namely, the set of points under which v is translation invariant, which is a subspace due to the
homogeneity of v), then
                               S(v) = {z ∈ Rn : Nv (z) = Nv (0)}.
From Corollary 3.4(iii), we know that Nv (0) = NV (Z) ≥ 1 + α. Thus, dim S(v) ̸∈ {n − 1, n}.
Indeed, if dim S(v) = n, then v is constant, yet we know v(0) = 0, which means that v ≡ 0, which
contradicts v not being identically zero. Also, if dim S(v) = n − 1, then graph(v) would be a union
of half-planes whose boundaries meet along an (n − 1)-dimensional subspace which would mean
Nv (0) = 1, contradicting Nv (0) ≥ 1 + α.

Thus, dim S(v) ∈ {0, 1, 2, . . . , n − 2}. We may therefore stratify BVQ as follows. For each Z ∈ BVQ ,
write BZ for the set of all coarse blow-ups constructed in the above manner by sequences of
rescalings of V centred at Z. Then, necessarily each v ∈ BZ is a homogeneous function in BV which
has degree of homogeneity ≥ 1+α and spine dimension at most n−2. For each j ∈ {0, 1, . . . , n−2},
set
                           Sj := {Z ∈ BVQ : dim S(v) ≤ j for all v ∈ BZ }.
Clearly by definition S0 ⊂ S1 ⊂ · · · ⊂ Sn−2 = BQ
                                                V . So far everything we have discussed has been
              Q
for any Z ∈ BV . We now claim that for each j ∈ {0, 1, 2, . . . , n − 2},

(3.21)                                 dimH (Sj ∩ (BV<2 ∪ BV>2 )) ≤ j.

Evidently (3.21) proves the proposition by taking j = n − 2. Proving (3.21) will follow by standard
arguments related to stratifications provided we can show that, for each Z ∈ BV<2 ∪ BV>2 , nearby
points with planar frequency ≥ NV (Z) − ε must concentrate around the spine of a blow-up in BZ
(for sufficiently small ε). Indeed, without loss of generality we may assume Z = 0 with the unique
tangent plane at Z being Q|P0 |, take any sequence σj ↓ 0 and again set Vj := (η0,σj )# V . Consider
any point z ∈ B1n (0) for which there exist Zj = (ζj , ξj ) singular points of Vj at which the (unique)
tangent cone is a multiplicity Q plane such that ξj → z, and NVj (Zj ) ≥ NV (Z) − δj for some
sequence δj ↓ 0. Then, from Corollary 3.6, we know that

                            Nv−ℓva ,z (z) ≥ lim sup NVj (Zj ) ≥ NV (Z) = Nv (0).
                                             j→∞

Thus, to complete the proof we need to show that this implies z ∈ S(v). To do this, we need
to show that Nv (z) = Nv (0), which from the above is equivalent to showing that ℓva ,z ≡ 0;
this is where we will need to use that NV (Z) ̸= 2. We split this into two cases (recall here
Nv (0) = NV (Z) ∈ [1 + α, ∞) \ {2}).

First, suppose that Nv (0) ∈ [1 + α, ∞) \ Z, i.e. Nv (0) is not an integer. Then if va ̸≡ 0, va would be
a harmonic function (cf. [BKMW25, (B2)]) which is homogeneous of some non-integer degree: this
is impossible, and thus we must have va ≡ 0. This of course then implies that ℓva ,z ≡ 0 for every
z ∈ B1n (0), and thus Nv−ℓva ,z (z) = Nv (z) ≥ Nv (0), implying that Nv (z) = Nv (0) and so z ∈ S(v).
This shows that Zk must be concentrating on S(v), which has dimension ≤ k.

Lastly, we just need to show this claim when Nv (0) ∈ {3, 4, . . . }. Here, we cannot argue that va ≡ 0.
Instead, we will argue that ℓva ,z ≡ 0 for the points z as above. Indeed, notice that here we have, by
assumption, that Nv−ℓva ,z (z) ≥ Nv (0) ≥ 3. This tells us that, as va is a smooth harmonic function,
that va −ℓva ,z vanishes to degree at least 2. Hence, 0 = Dx2 (va −ℓva ,z )(z) = Dx2 va (z), as ℓva ,z is linear
and so Dx2 ℓva ,z (x) ≡ 0. We now argue that this with the homogeneity of va implies Dva (z) = 0.
Indeed, consider for any i ∈ {1, . . . , n} the function w(t) := Di va (tz) for t > 0. Since D2 va (z) = 0,
we evidently have w′ (1) = 0. However, since va is homogeneous of degree Nv (0), we know that
             BRIAN KRUMMEL, PAUL MINTER, AND NESHAN WICKRAMASEKERA                                      39

Di va is homogeneous of degree Nv (0) − 1, and hence w(t) = tNv (0)−1 w(1) = tNv (0)−1 Di va (z).
But then w′ (t) = (Nv (0) − 1)tNv (0)−2 Di va (z), and hence 0 = w′ (1) = (Nv (0) − 1)Di va (z). As
Nv (0) − 1 ≥ 2, evidently we must have Di va (z) = 0, and hence as this is true for each i ∈ {1, . . . , n}
we see that Dva (z) = 0. But we can then of course repeat this argument (with w(t) = va (tz)), as
Nv (0) > 2, to similarly get that va (z) = 0. In particular, at such z we have ℓva ,z ≡ 0, and so again
Nv (z) = Nv−ℓva ,z (z) = Nv (0), and thus z ∈ S(v).

This therefore shows that the set Sk ∩(BV<2 ∪BV>2 ) has the desired subspace approximation property
in order to show that its Hausdorff dimension is at most k, completing the proof (cf. [Sim96, Section
3.4, Lemma 3]).                                                                                     □


Note: One sees that the above proof does not show that the set BV2 of points of planar frequency
exactly 2 has Hausdorff dimension ≤ n − 2. Indeed, one cannot show that ℓv,z ≡ 0 at the points z
in question, as it is possible for va − ℓva ,z to vanish to degree 2 (i.e. be of quadratic order) at every
point, e.g. if n = 2 and va (x, y) = x2 − y 2 . Indeed, if va is non-zero and homogeneous of degree 2,
then va − ℓva ,z must vanish to degree 2 at every point z (by Taylor’s theorem and the homogeneity).

Remark 3.8. Whilst the above proof does not control the size of BV2 , if for each n-dimensional
subspace P ⊂ Rn+k we set
                              BV2,P := {Z ∈ BV2 : TZ V = P }
then the proof does show that dimH (BV2,P ) ≤ n − 2, i.e. points where the planar frequency is 2 and
the tangent cone is a given plane has Hausdorff dimension at most n−2 (this follows because one can
arrange that the linear function ℓv,z arising at the end of the proof of Proposition 3.7 has vanishing
derivative, from which knowing the planar frequency is 2 is enough to conclude in the analogous
manner). In particular, if we knew that the stationary varifold V was represented by a Q-valued
Lipschitz graph with zero average, then the tangent plane at any branch point is P0 (say), and so
we can conclude that the whole branch set has Hausdorff dimension ≤ n − 2 at this stage with no
further argument needed. Thus, the only situation which we do not know how to address as of now
is when locally about a frequency 2 branch point, there is a whole continuum of frequency 2 branch
points all with different tangent planes. Note that also for this, the problematic case (if one was
arguing by contradiction, blowing-up around a point of positive Hs -upper density for some s > 0)
is when the coarse blow-up coincides with a multiplicity Q single-valued harmonic function which
is homogeneous of degree 2. To deal with this, we need a more complicated argument involving a
center manifold (see Section 4).

Remark 3.9. The above proof also has the following interesting consequence. Suppose V ∈ V
(for instance, we could take V = VL as in Corollary 1.7). Suppose Z ∈ BVQ is a singular point of V
where the (unique) tangent cone is Q|P0 |, and the planar frequency of V at Z is ̸= 2. Then, for all
δ > 0, there is a radius r > 0 (depending on V and δ) such that if Z̃ ∈ BV has planar frequency
NV (Z̃) ≥ NV (Z) − δ and |Z − Z̃| < r, then not only must Z̃ be within δ of a subspace of dimension
≤ n − 2, but also

(3.22)                               dist(Z̃, P0 ) ≤ δ ÊV,P0 (B2r (Z)).

Indeed, this follows from combining the proofs of Corollary 3.5 and Proposition 3.7, as indeed in
the proof of Proposition 3.7 we showed that the linear part of the average of the blow-up vanishes
at the limit of such points. Furthermore, these also give

(3.23)                   distH (TZ̃ V ∩ B1 (0), P0 ∩ B1 (0)) ≤ δ ÊV,P0 (B2r (Z)).
40                 BRANCH SET STRATIFICATION FOR STATIONARY VARIFOLDS

The inequality (3.22) should be compared to the corollary of the Lojasiewicz inequality which says
that for a (single-valued) minimal graph u, if Du(x) = 0 then there is a neighbourhood of x such
that any point y in this neighbourhood which obeys Du(y) = 0 necessarily also obeys u(y) = u(x).
Because points of non-integer planar frequency do not occur in the setting of single-valued minimal
graphs, both (3.22) and (3.23) are most directly comparable when the point has planar frequency an
integer ≥ 3. Indeed, for single-valued minimal graphs u, the Lojasiewicz inequality gives that at any
point with D2 u(x) = 0, there is a neighbourhood of x for which any point y in this neighbourhood
with D2 u(y) = 0 necessarily has Du(x) = Du(y), and hence also u(x) = u(y). Both (3.22) and
(3.23) are weakened versions of these facts for V ∈ V, which due to the branching behaviour are
not in general known to satisfy a version of the Lojasiewicz inequality.

4. Dimension Bound on Planar Frequency ≥ 2 Branch Points via Center Manifold
To study the set of points where the planar frequency is 2 (or even ≥ 2) we utilise a center manifold.
We wish to take some time to summarise the idea, in light of planar frequency. The basic philosophy
is that we wish to measure the frequency of V relative to a more general manifold M instead of
a plane; indeed, if M is a plane, our discussion will reduce to the planar frequency. The manifold
M needs to satisfy some conditions for this to be possible:
     (A) M needs to be C 3 . This is necessary to mimic the first variation arguments we used to show
         the approximate monotonicity of the planar frequency function, namely variations normal
         to M and variations within M. The latter involves differentiating a vector field whose flow
         lines match the geodesic arcs emitting from a point, and for this M needs to be C 3 .
     (B) M cannot be arbitrary, but needs to have some “variational structure” itself. This is perhaps
         expected if one wishes there to be a frequency function with a monotonicity property in
         the first place, as the monotonicity arises from control on suitable variational identities.
         This requirement manifests itself analytically when one needs to establish suitable doubling
         conditions of V relative to M (cf. Lemma 2.6). This doubling property is needed to control
         certain error terms arising from our variational identities, as we saw in Section 3 for the
         planar frequency.
     (C) M should (ideally12) touch V at all points of interest, namely any point of planar frequency
         ≥ 2. This guarantees that the frequency value is non-trivial at points of interest. Morally,
         one is then able to control the size of the set of points with planar frequency ≥ 2 by instead
         controlling the touching set of two surfaces (or at least the relevant part of the touching
         set), in an analogous spirit to classical quantitative unique continuation problems.
     (D) A more subtle condition arises for general M: there are certain error terms which arise in
         the first variation arguments which are linear in the average of the normal map representing
         V over M (assuming such a map exists); this is in addition to other errors which are, in
         theory, higher order. One needs to control such linear error terms in some manner. If M
         were a minimal surface itself, these linear errors would in fact vanish as they also involve the
         mean curvature of M. Likewise, if the normal map representing V over M had zero average,

12One could imagine a more complicated argument where the manifold M is allowed to change with the radius, such
that these manifolds converge in some strong way as the radius goes to 0 to a manifold which is tangent. One could
then measure the frequency with respect to these changing manifolds. Such a problem does arise in Almgren’s original
work when trying to use a center manifold for all branch points, which in our language corresponds to including also
branch points of planar frequency < 2. We however do not need to do this, as the planar frequency allows us to
deal with such points using blow-ups relative to tangent planes as we saw in Section 3. In some sense, the need for
changing the center manifold at a given point is replaced by a much simpler situation where one changes the “center
manifold” at nearby points instead (i.e. we take the “center manifold” at points of planar frequency < 2 to be the
tangent plane at the point).
               BRIAN KRUMMEL, PAUL MINTER, AND NESHAN WICKRAMASEKERA                                                 41

         then these terms would also vanish. It appears hard however to guarantee either of these
         situations, and thus for general M when this term does not vanish, we need to control it
         suitably in order to establish approximate monotonicity of the associated frequency function
         relative to M. In order to show the appropriate control, namely that this error is higher
         order (say, approximately cubic), M needs to somehow mirror (the average of) V ; for
         instance, at a point where V is close to a plane with some rate, M should be also. Whilst
         this ties in nicely to (C) above, it is crucial that everywhere M is a reflection of the decay
         rate of the varifold to its average, rather than just at a single point. This is important for
         certain estimates, which without one cannot control these linear error terms.
Typically in the literature the center manifold is described as being an “approximation to the
average of the sheets” of the varifold, with its use being to construct a blow-up which is non-
collapsed and thus has singularities; this is done by showing that the blow-up relative to the center
manifold has zero average, yet the full blow-up is non-zero (the latter being a result of having
a bounded frequency function with a monotonicity-type property). We stress that whilst this is
loosely the truth, the center manifold needs to be much more than just a manifold-approximation
to the average of the sheets in order for there to be an approximately monotone frequency function
with respect to it. Indeed, what is necessary is for the center manifold to be close to the average
whilst also mirroring the decay of the varifold towards its tangent plane(s), as described in (D) above.
This is much stronger than just being an approximation to the average, and this is consistent with
the center manifold needing to be an object which inherently has a variational structure built into it
in order for there to be an approximately monotone frequency function. Thus, “an approximation
to the average” is perhaps more accurately described as “an approximation to the average which
reflects the decay behaviour between the varifold and its average”. Again, the reason for this is
not to get a blow-up with zero average, but instead to show the approximate monotonicity of the
frequency relative to the center manifold at points of interest (indeed, in our approach, it is not the
fact that the average of the blow-up is zero which allows us to ultimately conclude our argument 13,
but only that the average of the graphical representation of the varifold over the center manifold is
suitably small in order to control the error terms needed to show the relevant frequency function is
approximately monotone). The size of the relevant singular set is then controlled by controlling an
appropriate subset of the touching set of V and the center manifold, in the same spirit as classical
quantitative unique continuation arguments. The average is a suitable candidate for building the
center manifold for another reason, namely because it is close to harmonic, and the error term in
this closeness is cubic in |Du| (which is the correct order we wanted to make these linear errors
involving the average). It is ultimately this fact that the error between the average and a harmonic
function is cubic which enables us to establish the regularity point from (A) above for our center
manifold.
Remark: In Almgren’s construction [Alm00] (and also in De Lellis–Spadaro [DLS16a]) the center
manifold construction is much more complicated than what we need here, as we only need the
center manifold to pass through points of planar frequency ≥ 2, which we will be able to guarantee.
If one tries to construct a center manifold which can be used for every branch point, it will not pass
through those branch points where the decay rate is < 2. (It also won’t necessarily pass through
density Q classical singularities, which one may think of as points with “frequency 1”).

13The average being zero is useful from the perspective of improved regularity, however. Indeed, knowing that you
have a non-trivial blow-up with zero average means that the blow-up cannot collapse into a single harmonic function
with multiplicity Q in the limit, and so it must ‘separate’. In principle, this separation then allows one to understand
further regularity structures for the varifold about branch points in an inductive fashion, using the reduction of
the multiplicity on the sheets of the blow-up. This is a key mechanism in the regularities theories first seen in
[Cha88, Wic14], for instance.
42              BRANCH SET STRATIFICATION FOR STATIONARY VARIFOLDS

Remark: It is perhaps instructive to consider how one establishes a dimension bound on flat
singular points which are not branch points, and why that case is significantly more simple. Indeed,
there the varifold is locally given by the sum of minimal graphs, and so one can reduce to bounding
the Hausdorff dimension of the touching set of two minimal graphs, say f and g, defined over
the (common) tangent plane. One can then look at the difference f − g, which solves an elliptic
PDE, for which one can use methods from [GL86] to establish directly a form of monotonicity of
a frequency function (this is also the philosophy taken by Simon and the third author in [SW16]).
Using elliptic estimates, the result then follows simply by blowing up. In the language of center
manifolds, one could view this argument in two ways. One could instead look at the two-valued
function Jf K + JgK and compare it to the average 21 (f + g) of the sheets, which would morally then
be looking at the two-valued symmetric function ± 12 Jf − gK; notice that this is then the same as
looking at the single-valued difference f − g. Alternatively, one could use the graph of g as the
“center manifold”, and measure the frequency of f relative to g; morally this would be looking at
f − g. However, even in this very simple situation the center manifold construction will not agree
with either of these “natural” choices, and as a result one has to deal with the extra linear error
term mentioned in (D). The center manifold is then built essentially to deal with this error term
when there is seemingly no other “natural” candidate for a manifold to measure frequency with
respect to. It would be interesting to investigate whether in some specialised situations, such as
area minimisers coming from calibrated submanifolds, one can find a more natural candidate, such
as another calibrated submanifold.

Remark 4.1. Loosely speaking, the center manifold can be described as follows: for each x ∈
B1n (0), let r(x) denote the largest radius σ > 0 such that both the height and tilt excess of V
relative to the tangent plane of ua at x fail to decay at a rate of 2 (or even 2 − δ for some small
δ > 0 fixed) in the cylinder Rk × Bσn (x); notice that r(x) = 0 if x is (the projection of) a flat
singular point of planar frequency ≥ 2. Then, the center manifold is morally the graph of

                                       ℘(x) := (ua ∗ ϱr(x) )(x)

where ϱ is a suitable radial bump function and ϱr(x) (y) := r(x)−n ϱ(y/r(x)); here, if r(x) = 0 we set
℘(x) := ua (x). Notice that such a function obeys the last three points above: provided r(x) is small
enough, ℘(x) is close to the average, which is almost harmonic, which would allow us to establish
(B). Point (C) is clear by definition. Finally, certainly this definition reflects the decay rate of V
to its average, so (D) holds. The regularity conclusion required in (A) is, however, unclear. In
our actual construction of a center manifold, we take a suitable discretisation of this definition,
which will enable us to show the regularity whilst also maintaining the other three conditions. We
also note that this heuristic definition also shows why one should expect Almgren’s observation
that when u is actually a single-valued function whose graph is minimal, then the center manifold
coincides with the graph of u and so provides an improved regularity estimate from C 1,α to C 3,β
(as in the single-valued case, the planar frequency is ≥ 2 everywhere, and so r(x) ≡ 0 for all x).

4.1. Construction of a center manifold. We break this down into several steps.
Step 1: Initial Set-Up and Discretisation. For j ∈ Z≥1 , write C j for the collection of (closed)
cubes L of P0 of the form

                                L = [a1 , a1 + 2ℓ] × · · · × [an , an + 2ℓ] ⊂ P0

where 2ℓ = 21−j =: 2ℓ(L) is the side-length of the cube L, ai ∈ 21−jSZ with ai ∈ [−4, 4 − 2ℓ]. The
center of L is xl := (a1 + ℓ, . . . , an + ℓ) ∈ Rn . We also write C := ∞    j
                                                                        j=1 C . If H, L ∈ C are such
that H ⊂ L, we call L an ancestor of H and H a descendant of L. If in addition ℓ(L) = 2ℓ(H), we
call H a child of L and L the parent of H. Furthermore, if L ∈ C j we say j is the level of L, and
write level(L) := j.
We now build a Whitney–style decomposition of [−4, 4]n which reflects the decay behaviour of V in
each region. Cubes will be chosen depending on the failure of decay of either the tilt-excess or the
height (in L∞ ) at suitable scales. We will have numerous choices in our construction, determined
by choices of constants. These constants are:
44                 BRANCH SET STRATIFICATION FOR STATIONARY VARIFOLDS

      (i) M0 ≥ 4, determining the scale of the balls determined by our Whitney-style decomposition,
          and thus how much overlap different balls have (thus M0 is a parameter determining the
          level of ‘discretisation’);
     (ii) N0 ≥ 1, determining how small the cubes we are looking at are by controlling the maximal
          cube size (morally, this gives an upper bound on the convolution scale by 2−N0 in Remark
          4.1, thus forcing a certain degree of closeness to the average);
     (iii) Ce ≥ 1 determining the failure of tilt-excess decay;
     (iv) Ch ≥ 1 determining the failure of L∞ height decay.
We will always be choosing M0 first depending on V (in particular, we take this to include the
dependence on n, k, Q, for simplicity), followed by N0 , then followed by Ce , then Ch , and finally
ε2 . In this ordering of constant choice, later constants are therefore allowed to depend on earlier
ones, e.g. Ce is allowed to depend on M0 and N0 , as in the end these will only depend on V. As an
initial requirement, we impose that M0 , N0 obey
                                              √
(4.1)                                     M0 n27−N0 ≤ 1.

Remark 4.2. One might wonder why we chose a power of 1 − 12           in condition (EX), as opposed to
the (seemingly) more natural power of 1. It is actually very important that this power is < 1, with
the reason being because one cannot distinguish between regular points and singular points when
the power is 1 (naturally, smooth functions come into their tangent planes at a rate of at least 2
by Taylor’s theorem). As such we do not want to see failure of condition (EX) because the sheets
locally coincide, and the rate of decay to the tangent plane is exactly 2; morally at such points the
center manifold should just be the sheet itself, but this would not necessarily happen if (EX) failed
at such points15. This technicality manifests itself when we try to prove the doubling condition for
cubes in (EX) (see Lemma 4.19) analogously to what we did in Lemma 2.6, except now we will want
to control the whole energy by that of the average-free part. But one cannot do this if the sheets
all coincide, as the average-free part will vanish! And the issue is exactly because the average could
14This is a slight abuse of notation, as n does not denote a dimension here but rather indicates ‘neighbouring’ cubes.
15Compare this to the single-valued case. One would want to construct a center manifold which coincides with the
graph itself (indeed, it was already noted by Almgren that his construction achieves this). In this situation, (HT)
always fails. However, if the power in (EX) was 1, there could be points where (EX) holds – even though the graph
is smooth! To get around this, we decrease the power slightly, and this issue disappears.
             BRIAN KRUMMEL, PAUL MINTER, AND NESHAN WICKRAMASEKERA                                   45

have decay rate exactly 2. The choice of power 11  12 is, however, essentially arbitrary: any power in
(2/3, 1) would suffice, and we choose 11/12 simply because it makes some of the constants nicer;
we will make it clear in our statements where this choice appears. The upper bound of 1 here is
exactly for the above reason, whilst the lower bound of 2/3 is the needed to prove that the center
manifold is C 3,β for some β > 0. As a final comment, one might think that if one takes an exponent
of 1 in (EX), one could rule out coincidence with a single-valued function in an alternative manner
by taking the constant Ce large enough (as a homogeneous degree 2 harmonic function with energy
1 at unit scale cannot have proportionally large energy at a smaller scale). However, the problem
is more a result of the relative change in the tilt-excess from one scale to the next, which is what
one needs for the doubling condition in Lemma 4.19 (which is unchanged by changing Ce ), rather
than the size of the constant.

Morally, at each stage condition (NN) forces all neighbouring cubes to those included at the previous
stage in S to “spawn” and be included in S; they might not lie in Snj (as they might lie in Sej or
Shj ), but they will lie in S j . It is this inductive spawning, combined with possibly smaller cubes
appearing from (EX) S   and (HT), which gives rise to S. The complement of this is then Γ. Note also
that for each j, Rj ∪ i≤j S i = [−4, 4]n .
Our first observation is that the pair (Γ, S) forms a (weak ) Whitney decomposition of [−4, 4]n , in
the sense that:
 (W1) Γ is closed, Γ ∪ L∈S L = [−4, 4]n , and Γ ∩ L = ∅ for all L ∈ S;
                        S

 (W2) if L1 , L2 ∈ S are distinct, then int(L1 ) ∩ int(L2 ) = ∅, where int(L) denotes the interior of
      the (closed) cube L;
 (W3) if L1 , L2 ∈ S have non-empty intersection, then |level(L1 ) − level(L2 )| ≤ 1; in particular,
      ℓ(L1 )/ℓ(L2 ) ∈ { 21 , 1, 2}.
We leave the reader to verify these simple properties. It can also be seen that (W1) – (W3) imply
that for every L ∈ S we have dist(Γ, L) ≥ 2ℓ(L) (we stress that here for two sets A, B ⊂ Rn we
define dist(A, B) := inf a∈A, b∈B |a − b|, which is different from the Hausdorff distance distH (A, B)).
However, we do not necessarily have any bound of the form dist(Γ, L) ≤ Cℓ(L) (such a bound
would mean that (Γ, S) is a Whitney decomposition of [−4, 4]n in the more traditional sense, hence
why we called the above a ‘weak’ Whitney decomposition).
Step 2: Initial Comparisons between Decay Regions. The decomposition (Γ, S) is the
decomposition we want which reflects the decay behaviour of V . Let us now prove some basic
properties regarding it.

Lemma 4.3. There exists ε = ε(V) ∈ (0, 1) and C ∗ = C ∗ (V, M0 , N0 ) ∈ (0, ∞) such that if ε2 < ε,
Ce ≥ C ∗ , and Ch ≥ C ∗ Ce , then S j = ∅ for all j ≤ N0 + 6.

The next lemma gives comparisons between the planes PL realising ÊV (BL ) for different cubes

relevant region.

Proof. The proof is a bit long but is effectively bookkeeping. The idea is that, since our cube
refinement only stops when the decay condition stops, we still have control on each cube as they
become finer and finer.

Notice first that the second inclusion in (4.3) we have already shown in the proof of Lemma 4.3;
note that the same proof there works for any L ∈ R ∪ S. Notice also that the second claims in
(iii) and (iv), respectively, follow from the first claims in (iii) and (iv), respectively, by choosing
C2 ε2 < 1, i.e. ε2 = ε2 (C2 ) is sufficiently small. Thus, to prove (4.3) we only need to prove the first
inclusion in (4.3), and to prove (iii) and (iv) we just need to prove the first inequality of each.

Consider first situation (a), i.e. when H ⊂ L (this includes (4.3)). We start by proving claims (4.3),
(i), (ii), and (iii) by induction on the level of H (we will return to (iv) afterwards). The base case
is when level(H) = N0 ; this forces H = L and so level(L) = N0 also. In particular, as H = L, the
first inclusion in (4.3) is trivial, as is claim (i). To see (ii), as ℓ(H) = 2−N0 , the argument from
the proof of Lemma 4.3 will give that ∥πPH − πP0 ∥ ≤ C̄ ÊV for some C̄ = C̄(V, M0 , N0 , Ce ), which
proves (ii). For the first claim in (iii), notice that

(indeed, |xH | + 32rH ≤ 5 n holds because |xH | ≤ 4 n and rH ≤ 2−7 ). Thus, as by assumption

             hV (C32rH (pH , P0 )) ≤ hV,P0 (C5√n (0, P0 )) ≤ C(V)ÊV = 2N0 C(V) · ÊV ℓ(H)

as ℓ(H) = 2−N0 . This proves (iii) when level(H) = N0 .

Thus, under the assumption of (a), we have verified (4.3) and (i) – (iii) when level(H) = N0 . Now
we move to the inductive step.

First consider (4.3). Notice again that when H = L, the first inclusion in (4.3) is trivial, and
so we may assume H ⊊ L. We can therefore find H ≡ H i ⊂ H i−1 ⊂ · · · ⊂ H j ≡ L such that
i > j and H k ∈ Rk for all k < i (we make no claim on whether H i ≡ H is in Ri or S i ). It

We may now prove from relatively simple parts of the above lemmas that both the tilt-excess and
height remain controlled on all the balls BL , for L ∈ R ∪ S. This is perhaps expected since as soon

Step 3: Graphical Representation in Decay Regions and Regularity Estimates. Since
we have control over the tilt-excess and the height over cubes in our Whitney-style decomposition,
for suitable choices of parameter we will be able to apply our ε-regularity theorem (Theorem 2.2)
to deduce a certain graphical representation holds over the cube regions, with estimates reflecting
the decay of V on the given cube to a plane. We will then use these graphs to construct the center
manifold. In fact, we also prove more to allow us to compare the graphs over neighbouring cubes.

Lemma 4.6. Let C ∗ be as in Lemma 4.3 and Ce ≥ C ∗ , Ch ≥ C ∗ Ce . Then, for sufficiently small
ε2 = ε2 (V, M0 , N0 , Ce , Ch ) ∈ (0, 1), the following holds. Let H, L ∈ R ∪ S be such that either:
   (a) H ⊂ L; or
   (b) H ∩ L ̸= ∅ and level(L) ≤ level(H) ≤ level(L) + 1.

mass assumption in Theorem 2.2 holds for V C32rL (pL , PH ). But then from Lemma 4.4(iv) we

where Ce = eC(V, M0 , N0 , Ce , Ch ). Indeed, the bounds in (II) come from the bounds on the tilt-

as Lemma 4.4(iv) so that we know spt∥V ∥ ∩ C32rL (pL , PH ) ⊂ BL (conclusion (I) above also uses
this).
These functions will form the building blocks of our center manifold construction as well as for the
comparison between V and the center manifold. We call uHL the (H, L)-representation of V .
We now begin building the center Rmanifold from these building blocks. First, fix ϱ ∈ Cc∞ (B1n (0)) a
non-negative radial function with Rn ϱ = 1. For λ > 0, we write ϱλ (x) := λ−n ϱ(x/λ).
For each H, L ∈ R ∪ S as in Lemma 4.6, given the corresponding (H, L)-representation uHL of V ,
write
                                   hHL := (uHL )a ∗ ϱℓ(L)
for the smoothed average, i.e. we convolute by an amount proportional to the side-length of the cube
the function was constructed relative to (cf. Remark 4.1). We call hHL the tilted (H, L)-convoluted
average (“tilted” because it is defined over a subset of PH , not of P0 ).
Now, as the Lipschitz constant of hHL is bounded by C̃ ÊV ℓ(L) (from Lemma 4.6), and more-
over since ∥πPH − πP0 ∥ ≤ C1 ÊV (from Lemma 4.4(ii)), we may find a (smooth) function gHL :
  n (x , P ) → Rk such that

                                                              n
                                                                L
                                                                  (pL , PH )).

We call gHL the (H, L)-convoluted average.
Remark: In the special case H = L, we will call uL ≡ uLL the L-representation of V , hL ≡ hLL
the tilted L-convoluted average, and gL ≡ gLL the L-convoluted average.
It is the functions gL which we will glue together to form the center manifold. In order to deduce
(1) the relevant regularity of the gluing; and (2) that the center manifold is close to the average of
V in a very strong way, we will prove some estimates regarding the gHL . The key fact for all this is
that a multi-valued minimal graph representing a stationary integral varifold close to a plane has
an average which is close to being harmonic, where the error term is cubic (it is this cubic nature
of the error which will give us the correct estimates for both (1) and (2) here). More precisely:

via certain interior Schauder estimates in the following lemma. We begin by comparing functions
defined on subsets of the same plane.

Whilst Lemma 4.9 controlled convoluted averages which are defined on the same plane, we also
need to compare the convoluted averages which are defined on planes corresponding to adjacent
cubes. This is needed to understand the regularity of the center manifold when we move from
cube-to-cube. This is achieved in the following lemma.

For (iii), notice that if we applied the argument for (ii) instead assuming that L is the parent of
H, we would deduce that for any 0 < γ < β < 3/4 we have

                              [D3 gH − D3 gL ]γ,BrH (xH ) ≤ Cβ,γ ÊV ℓ(H)β−γ .

Hence (taking e.g. γ = β/2)

        |D3 gH (xH ) − D3 gL (xL )| ≤ |D3 gH (xH ) − D3 gH (xL )| + |D3 gH (xL ) − D3 gL (xL )|
                                    ≤ ℓ(H)γ [D3 gH − D3 gL ]γ,BrH (xH ) + |D3 gH (xL ) − D3 gL (xL )|
                                    ≤ ℓ(H)γ · Cβ,β/2 ÊV ℓ(H)β−γ + C3/4−β ÊV ℓ(H)β
                                    ≡ Cβ ÊV ℓ(L)β .

If we iterate this and use the triangle inequality, we also get that for any ancestor L of H, for any
β ∈ (0, 3/4),
                              |D3 gH (xH ) − D3 gL (xL )| ≤ Cβ ÊV ℓ(L)β .
But now given any H, L ∈ P j , let H̃ and L̃ be the first ancestors of H and L with the same level
and non-empty intersection. The above bound combined with (ii) then gives

        |D3 gH (xH ) − D3 gL (xL )| ≤ |D3 gH (xH ) − D3 gH̃ (xH̃ )|
                                           + |D3 gH̃ (xH̃ ) − D3 gL̃ (xL̃ )| + |D3 gL̃ (xL̃ ) − D3 gL (xL )|
60                 BRANCH SET STRATIFICATION FOR STATIONARY VARIFOLDS

                                     ≤ Cβ ÊV ℓ(L̃)β .

But one can check from the cube construction that |xL −xH | ≥ c(n)ℓ(L̃), which therefore completes
the proof of (iii) (one changes β to 3/4 − β to get the exact form of (iii)).
To see (iv), the first bound follows simply from the height bound in the application of Theorem
2.2 which gave hH (see Lemma 4.6 and the subsequent discussion) and then changing coordinates
to gH (see [DLS16a, Lemma B.1]). Moreover, also from the application of Theorem 2.2 in Lemma
4.6, we know that
                              ∥DhH ∥C 0 (B6r (pH ,PH )) ≤ C ÊV ℓ(H)11/12
                                                 H

and thus the second inequality in (iv) follows immediately, with πP hH in place of πPxgH . But then
                                                                     x
the graph of gH over B4rH (xH , P0 ) is a subset of the graph of hH over B5rH (pH , PH ), and so the
tangent planes coincide on this region; thus, the result with πPxgH follows.
Finally we prove (v). Fix L ∈ S j . For i ≥ j, let P i (L) denote the set of all cubes in P i which
intersect L. If L̃ is as in (v), then noting that if H ∈ P i intersects L̃ it must be in P i (L), we have
by definition of ℘i

where we have used the fact that the number of cubes in P i (L) is bounded by some constant
depending only on n.                                                                     □

Finally, we can construct our center manifold from the ℘j .

Theorem 4.12 (Existence of Center Manifold). Let C ∗ be as in Lemma 4.3 and Ce ≥ C ∗ , Ch ≥

          j, k ≥ i + 2;
     (iii) ℘j converges in C 3,β , for any β ∈ (0, 3/4), to a function ℘ : [−4, 4]n → R which is C 3,γ for
           any γ ∈ (0, 3/4).

C(V, M0 , N0 , Ce , Ch ); this shows that ℘j converges uniformly to some function ℘ which is continu-

for all β ∈ (0, 3/4), and the limiting function ℘ is in C 3,γ for all γ ∈ (0, 3/4), hence proving (iii).

either H = L or H is a child of L. Now, if level(L) ≤ j − 2, we know from (ii) that ℘j (x) = ℘j+1 (x)
and so there is nothing to prove. So we may assume level(L) ∈ {j − 1, j}. But then from (i) and

                     ≤ C ÊV · 2−j + C ÊV · 2−j
                     ≡ C ÊV · 2−j

where we have bounded |yH − yL | using Lemma 4.4. Hence, taking the supremum over all x we get
∥℘j − ℘j+1 ∥C 0 ≤ C ÊV 2−j , as desired. This completes the proof.                         □

This completes the construction of the center manifold. We stress that we have built into the
construction of the center manifold the decay structure of V towards its tangent planes (which
should be compared with the method used to control error2 in the proof of Theorem 3.2). This will
be crucial for later estimates regarding frequency. Our next aim is to write V as a graph over the
center manifold, and use this decay structure of V to deduce several estimates for this graph. We
will always assume the assumptions of Theorem 4.12 from now on.
4.2. Construction of normal graph and its decay properties. Let M be the center manifold
given by Theorem 4.12. Since M is C 2 , we have the standard differential-geometric fact that there
exists a tubular neighbourhood U of M on which there exists an orthogonal projection map. More
precisely, there exists an open set U ⊃ M of Rn+k such that for each x ∈ U there is a unique point
y ∈ M with |x − y| < 1 and x − y ⊥ Ty M (of course M currently is only a subset of the cylinder
Rk × [−4, 4]n and we are only interested in the orthogonal projection restricted to this cylinder).
We write p(x) := y for this uniquely determined point, and thus p : U → M is the orthogonal
projection map. Moreover, since M is in fact C 3,1/2 with its C 3,1/2 norm (i.e. that of the graphing
function ℘) controlled by C ÊV , provided ε2 is sufficiently small we may assume that p is a C 2,1/2
map, and moreover that spt∥V ∥ ⊂ U.
             BRIAN KRUMMEL, PAUL MINTER, AND NESHAN WICKRAMASEKERA                                    63

Before constructing the graph over M representing V , we first give a lemma regarding the touching
set of V and M. We show that M touches V at every point of Φ(Γ), i.e. the image of the set
Γ in our Whitney decomposition used to form M, and moreover every flat singular point of V
with planar frequency ≥ 2 lies in Φ(Γ). In particular, M touches V at every flat singular point
of planar frequency ≥ 2. Recall that as V is represented by a GC 1,α function u over P0 , we have
Bu≥2 = πP0 (BV≥2 ) (here Bu≥2 means the obvious thing, namely branch points of u where the decay
rate to the tangent plane is ≥ 2).

Notice that Lemma 4.15(i) already tells us that V must be a Q-valued graph over M, which
moreover must be GC 1,α due to Theorem 2.2 and the C 3 regularity of M. Moreover, Lemma

our construction is that on each cube we can control the average of the normal graph, and moreover
the average of the normal graph is higher order (namely, it is higher order than quadratic) when
compared to the overall graph. This is made precise in the following theorem and is one of the
crucial aspects of building the center manifold the way we did (as this will be needed to control
certain error terms later on; the other crucial aspects are the doubling-style conditions coming from
Lemma 4.17 and Lemma 4.19).

This concludes the “new” estimates we will need to use the center manifold in the first place,
namely the smallness of the average in a very controlled way. Next, we need to establish more
“standard” estimates, which mirror those seen in Section 3 when dealing with the error terms
from the planar frequency function. In Section 3, we found regions where certain decay conditions
stopped and used an appropriate Vitali cover and a doubling condition to control the error term
error∗2 therein. Here, the regions where the decay stops have already been built into our center
manifold construction: these are the cubes in S. Since the center manifold was not constructed
“randomly”, but is built from ua , which itself is close to a harmonic function, we can therefore
establish the equivalent estimates and doubling conditions for the normal map N . The argument is
somewhat more technical, as M is not flat and moreover there are three types of cubes to consider,
but the reader should note the similarity in this part of the argument to that used for planar
frequency.
Let us start with the simplest case, namely the height cubes. The main goal is to control in every
height cube the size of the average-free part of N from below by the length of the respective cube;
this is a stronger form of a doubling-type condition as it is an L∞ statement rather than an L2
statement. This is achieved in (ii) of the following lemma:

Lemma 4.17 (Height Cube Control). There is a constant C̄ ∗ = C̄ ∗ (M0 ) ∈ (0, ∞) such that the
following holds. Suppose that Ch ≥ C̄ ∗ Ce . Then, if ε2 = ε2 (V, M0 , N0 , Ce , Ch ) ∈ (0, 1) is sufficiently
small, then the following hold for every L ∈ Sh :
                                        n+k

     (iii) L ∩ H = ∅ for every H ∈ Sn with ℓ(H) ≤ 12 ℓ(L).

The next case we analyse is that of the neighbouring cubes, i.e. Sn . Notice that Lemma 4.17(iii)
tells us that if H ∈ Sn then H must in fact spawn from a cube in Se , as it cannot be adjacent to
any larger cube in Sh . From this, we can deduce the following fact, morally saying that any cube
in Sn is “covered” by a cube in Se . Indeed, Lemma 4.17(iii) gives that if H ∈ Sn , then we can
find a chain L0 , L1 , . . . , Lj ≡ H such that L0 ∈ Se , Li ∈ Sn for all i ≥ 1, and Li ∩ Li−1 ̸= ∅ with
level(Li ) = level(Li−1 ) + 1 for all i ≥ 1. In particular, for such a chain we have

                                       H ⊂ B3√nℓ(L0 ) (xL0 , P0 ).

Now fix an ordering on the (countable) set Se such that the level is increasing along the ordering.
For H ∈ Sn , we then choose L0 ∈ Se minimal in this ordering for which there is a chain as just
described starting at L0 and ending at H. We call L0 the spawner of H. We may therefore partition
Sn into disjoint sets Sn (J), for J ∈ Se , where Sn (J) is the set of all H ∈ Sn whose spawner is J.
We call Sn (J) the spawners of J.

The following lemma regarding the spawner of H ∈ Sn is a simple consequence of the analysis so
far. Here, for q ∈ M we write Br (q) for the geodesic ball in M of radius r centred at q. The reason
we need such a lemma is because currently we do not know any decay property for the cubes in
Sn , and so we will use the spawner to control them, and so we need to know the relation between
H and its spawner.

error terms by those involved in the definition of the frequency function.

4.3. Frequency relative to center manifold. We now combine all the estimates from the pre-
vious subsection to show that the normal map N has an associated frequency function (namely,
the frequency of V relative to M) which is approximately monotone. We now fix choices of
M0 , N0 , Ce , Ch , depending only on V, so that the results of the previous sections hold.

Let φ : [0, ∞] → [0, 1] be the same function from Section 3. For each r ∈ (0, 3] we then define
                                             Z          
                                                    d(p)
                               D(r) := r 2−n
                                                φ          |DN (p)|2 dp
                                              M      r

and                                                    Z                  
                                                            ′       d(p)
                                   H(r) := −r    1−n

where d(p) denotes the geodesic distance in M between p and 0 ∈ M. Here, N is the normal map
to M given by Theorem 4.12. Note that these are the corresponding expressions to those defined
in Section 3 (up to controlled factors) except relative to M rather than a plane. When H(r) > 0,
we define the center manifold frequency function by

Remark: We will show later in Remark 4.26 that indeed H(r) > 0 for all r > 0: this will follow in
an identical fashion to that seen in Section 3, once we are able to control certain extra error terms
which arise in the equivalent choice of test function in the first variation.

Just like with the planar frequency, where we wanted to rule out an infinite order of contact between
V and the plane, here we want to rule out that V has an infinite order of contact with M, for which
we will establish an approximate monotonicity of the frequency function I(r). The argument will
mirror that of the planar case, except with more error terms due to M being potentially curved.

Throughout, we will write ∂r̂ for the derivative with respect to arc-length along geodesics in M
starting at 0. Let exp denote the exponential map T0 M ⊃ B3 → M. We will also write Br (0) for
the geodesic ball in M of radius r and centred at 0.

The main work that remains is to bound the error terms Erroj and Errin      j . Note that “error” is
only a reasonable name for these terms if they are actually smaller than the main terms, i.e. those
involving D(r) and H(r). As D(r) and H(r) are quadratic in N or DN , we see that the worse error
terms a priori are Erro1 and Errin
                                 1 , as these are only linear in N (notice that if M were minimal,
as was the case for planar frequency when M was taken to be a plane, these terms would vanish).
However, since they are in fact linear in Na , we hope to control them by making Na small. This is
exactly the reason for the center manifold construction. We will bound the errors in terms of D(r)
and H(r), which is why we needed to control both the tilt and the height in the center manifold
construction, and moreover why we had to be delicate with ensuring that Na is always of a higher
order than D(r) and H(r) (cf. Theorem 4.16), which we did by building the center manifold over
regions where decay stops.
When estimating the error terms, we will need to pass from the domain of integration, namely
Br (0), to the region in the plane B := πP0 (Br (0)) where we had our Whitney decomposition
forming M. Notice that as ∥℘∥C 3,1/2 ≤ Cε2 (see Theorem 4.12) we know that B is a C 2 convex
set which moreover has the property that, for all z ∈ ∂B there is a ball Br/2 (y, P0 ) ⊂ B with
B r/2 (y, P0 ) ∩ ∂B = {z}.
We therefore need to look at the Whitney regions that intersect B and control the error terms
across them. Analogously to the situation in Section 3, in order to reintroduce the function φr in
76                BRANCH SET STRATIFICATION FOR STATIONARY VARIFOLDS

our estimates, we need to be able to choose smaller balls which stay away from ∂B by a definite
amount compared to the size of the Whitney region: for this we use our doubling-style conditions,
such as Lemma 4.19 in the case of excess cubes Se . Morally, in Section 3 we only had to do this for
excess cubes Se , whereas here we now need to also look at Sh and Sn . The argument is essentially
analogous for Sh to that of Se (easier, in fact, as there is always a definite amount of separation),
and so Sh does not cause any significant complication. For Sn , however, we do not have an a priori
decay-change, and so we need to take the additional step at looking at its spawner, which has a
decay-change, and use this with Lemma 4.19. The complication is that, whilst the spawner is in Se ,
it might not lie inside B, and so we need to be careful choosing the ball for the doubling condition.
For such cubes we will need a slight modification, as we shall see.
So, we define a family of cubes T ⊂ Se ∪ Sh corresponding to each Whitney region intersecting B
as follows:
     (i) T contains all L ∈ Se ∪ Sh which intersect B;
     (ii) if L′ ∈ Sn intersects B, then L ∈ T , where L ∈ Se is the spawner of H.
                                                                                  √
By Lemma 4.18 and Lemma 4.13 we know that ℓ(L) ≤ 643r       √ and dist(L, B) ≤ 3 nℓ(L) if L ∈ T .

We now define a countable family F ⊂ T of cubes such that {B L }L∈F is a maximal pairwise
disjoint collection of balls. For this, first write S := supL∈T s(L) and select a maximal pairwise
disjoint subcollection F1 ⊂ T of cubes with s(L) ≥ S/2; clearly F1 is finite. Then, inductively at
stage k ≥ 2, select a maximal subcollection Fk ⊂ T such that {B L }L∈Fk are pairwise disjoint, these
                                           L̃                                         −k   1−k
balls do not intersect any ofS the balls B with L̃ ∈ F1 ∪ · · · ∪ Fk−1 , and s(L) ∈ [2 S, 2 S) for
L ∈ Fk . We then set F := k Fk . As F is a countable union of finite sets, we may fix an ordering
F = {Li }i∈N of F such that s(Li ) is decreasing.
Of course, we may then partition the cubes of S which intersect B into disjoint families {S(Li )}Li ∈F
depending on the associated ball which is intersected. Indeed, let H ∈ S have B ∩ H ̸= ∅. Then:
     (a) if H ∈ Se ∪ Sh , then H ∈ T , and so there is i ∈ N with B H ∩ B Li ̸= ∅. Choose i minimal
         with this property and set H ∈ S(Li ).

     (b) if H ∈ Sn , then its spawner J obeys J ∈ Se ∩ T . Hence, there is i ∈ N with B J ∩ B Li ̸= ∅.
         Choose i minimal with this property and set H ∈ S(Li ).

For each Li ∈ T , by definition of B Li we can clearly choose a ball Bℓ(Li )/4 (x̃, P0 ) ⊂ B Li ∩ B which

choice of ball by B(Li ). To summarise, we have the following properties of F = {Li }i∈N :
    (I) if Li ∈ F , then Li ∈ Se ∪ Sh , and Li has an associated ball B(Li ) of radius ℓ(Li )/4 which
        obeys B(Li ) ⊂ B Li ∩ B, dist(B(Li ), ∂B) ≥ ℓ(Li )/4, and whose center lies on the line
        segment connecting xLi to 0;

   (II) if i ̸= j, then B Li ∩ B Lj = ∅, and so in particular B(Li ) ∩ B(Lj ) = ∅ and Li , Lj are distinct;

        families S(Li ) are disjoint, i.e. S(Li ) ∩ S(Lj ) = ∅ for i ̸= j.

The last ingredient we need in order to bound the error terms correctly is to provide a way to
control the different error terms. For this, we will control the scales ℓ(Li ) for the family of cubes
F = {Li }i∈N described previously. As we can relate all the relevant quantities to these scales, this
will provide us with a convenient way to control the error terms in terms of the quantities D(r)
(and H(r)). We stress again, this works because we have built into our center manifold the failure
of the decay of tilt and height.

C = C(V, δ). We will remove this assumption shortly with an appropriate choice of δ.

Remark 4.26. We can now show that H(r) > 0 is always true. Indeed, if H(r) = 0 for some r,
then |N | ≡ 0 on Br (0) \ Br/2 (0), which in turn gives that E(r) = 0. In fact, if H(r) = 0, then all
conclusions of Lemma 4.24 go through without any assumption on a lower bound for I(r), and thus
all the above computations hold. Thus, (4.22) still holds (which we note comes from the analogous
variation as that used in the usual ‘squash inequality’, which gives the equivalent conclusion for
the planar frequency) and hence we would have
                                                                   2
                                                             1+ 3(n+3)
                                   D(r) ≤ C ÊV D(r)                     + C Ê2V r2 Σ(r).
84                 BRANCH SET STRATIFICATION FOR STATIONARY VARIFOLDS

But we also have from the first inequality in Lemma 4.24 (as H(r) = 0) that Σ(r) ≤ CD(r), and
hence we get
                                               1+ 2
                            D(r) ≤ C ÊV D(r) 3(n+3) + C Ê2V r2 D(r).
Thus, if ÊV is sufficiently small, rearranging this would give that D(r) ≤ 0, i.e. D(r) = 0, and so
|DN | = 0 on Br (0). But as |N | ≡ 0 on Br (0)\Br/2 (0), this would mean that |N | ≡ 0 on Br (0), i.e. V
coincides with QJBr (0)K here. But as M was a C 3,1/2 manifold, this would contradict 0 ∈ sing(V ).
Thus we have reached the desired contradiction, and so H(r) > 0 for all r > 0. In particular, I(r)
is well-defined for all r > 0 (and again we stress this is true without assuming any lower bound on
I(r)).

We can now prove the first fact about the center manifold frequency, which is a growth-bound. In
particular, this gives that supr I(r) < ∞. We will use this in turn to establish a uniform lower
bound on I(r), i.e. inf r I(r) > 0, giving our choice of δ above, and then finally we will use this to
show that I(r) is approximately monotone in r.

Theorem 4.27 (Frequency Growth Bound). If ε2 = ε2 (V) ∈ (0, 1) is sufficiently small, then for
all 0 < a ≤ b < 1,
                                                               4

Here, C = C(V).

Remark: Clearly Theorem 4.27 gives supr∈(0,1) I(r) ≤ eC ÊV (1 + I(1)) < ∞.

Proof. Set Ω(r) := log(max{I(r), 1}). To prove the theorem, it suffices to show that
                                                                4

loss of generality assume that Ω > 0 on (a, b), i.e. I(r) > 1, as this case will imply the full result.

Next, we use Theorem 4.27 to establish a fixed lower bound on the center manifold frequency. In
fact, we will be able to show that if ε2 = ε2 (V) is sufficiently small, then I(r) ≥ 1/2 for all r (the
choice of a lower bound is 1/2 is arbitrary, and a lower bound of 1 − η could be achieved provided
ε2 is allowed to depend on η ∈ (0, 1) as well).

Corollary 4.28 (Uniform Frequency Lower Bound). Provided ε2 = ε2 (V) is sufficiently small, we
have I(r) ≥ 1/2 for all r > 0.

Remark 4.29. Currently, all our arguments have been based at the origin, i.e. defining the center
manifold frequency at 0. We can also base oursevles at different points of planar frequency ≥ 2
and the above arguments will also work, which allows us to define the center manifold frequency
function Iy (r) at different points of planar frequency ≥ 2. Corollary 4.28 applies at these other
base points as well, provided ε2 = ε2 (V) ∈ (0, 1) is sufficiently small, to provide a uniform lower
bound.

Now note that one can rerun the entire argument from Theorem 4.27 using the uniform lower
bound 1/2 provided by Corollary 4.28. Indeed, in the proof of Theorem 4.27 instead define Ω(r) :=
log(max{I(r), 1/2}) ≡ log I(r), and then the whole argument leading to (⋆) goes through, which in
                                         4
                                      3(n+3)
turn gives I(a) ≤ eC ÊV b                      I(b) for any 0 < a ≤ b < 1. Thus, we have now established:

Theorem 4.30 (Approximate Monotonicity of Center Manifold Frequency). If ε2 = ε2 (V) ∈ (0, 1)
is sufficiently small, then for any 0 < a ≤ b < 1,
                                                                                  4
                                                                        C ÊV b 3(n+3)
                                                          I(a) ≤ e                       I(b).

Here, C = C(V) ∈ (0, ∞).

In particular, we now get that the limit I(0) := limr↓0 I(r) must exist (and is ≥ 1/2 > 0). In fact,
the argument in Corollary 4.28 now gives, for any ε∗ > 0,

                                                                          H(r)
                                                            lim inf                   > 0.
                                                              r↓0       r2I(0)+ε∗

But then since H(r) ≤ C Ê2V r4 , we get that we need I(0) ≥ 2. We therefore have:

Corollary 4.31. If ε2 = ε2 (V) ∈ (0, 1) is sufficiently small, then I(0) ≥ 2.

Remark 4.32. In fact, one has I(0) ≥ N (0), i.e. the center manifold frequency is at least as large
as the planar frequency at the point. See Remark 4.39 for the justification. We note that it is
                   BRIAN KRUMMEL, PAUL MINTER, AND NESHAN WICKRAMASEKERA                                                  89

entirely possible that I(0) = N (0), as is the case at any point where the planar frequency is not an
integer.

by Corollary 4.28 as well as Lemma 4.24 and (4.20) gives that, provided ε2 = ε2 (V) ∈ (0, 1) is

Note: Theorem 4.30 gives that for all r = r(V) sufficiently small, we have (for instance) I(r) ≥ 3/2.

These are the key facts we need to know regarding the center manifold frequency function in order
to conclude our argument. In particular, the approximate monotonicity of frequency provided by
Theorem 4.30, rather than the growth control given by Theorem 4.27, will allow us to show that
blow-ups relative to the center manifold are homogeneous of degree I(0).

scales, thus controlling its full W 1,2 norm by its L2 norm at those scales.

4.4. Center manifold blow-up and conclusion. We are now in a position to perform our blow-
up/linearisation procedure relative to the center manifold M from Theorem 4.12. Again, we have
fixed constants M0 = M0 (V), N0 = N0 (V, M0 ), Ce = Ce (V, M0 , N0 ), Ch = Ch (V, M0 , N0 , Ce ), and
                BRIAN KRUMMEL, PAUL MINTER, AND NESHAN WICKRAMASEKERA                                                     91

ε2 = ε2 (V, M0 , N0 , Ce , Ch ) such that all the previous results of the section hold. In particular,
ε2 = ε2 (V) only depends on V.

Recall that V ∈ V is such that 0 ∈ BV≥2 is a density Q branch point with planar frequency ≥ 2 and
whose (unique) tangent cone is Q|P0 |; this is of course without loss of generality,
                                                                               √ by translating
and rotating V . Moreover, by rescaling V we may also assume that at scale 6 n the conclusions
of the previous sections hold. Let M be the corresponding center manifold given by Theorem 4.12,
with N the associated normal map representing V over M, given by Theorem 4.16.

Now, take any sequence rj ↓ 0, and let sj ∈ ( 32 rj , 3rj ) be the radius from Corollary 4.34 corre-
sponding to rj . Consider the rescaled sequences

                                       Vj := (η0,sj )# V,         Mj := η0,sj (M),

and Nj : Mj → AQ (Rn+k ) given by Nj (p) := s−1
                                              j N (sj p). Clearly from the uniqueness of the
tangent cone to V at 0 we have Vj ⇀ Q|P0 |, and moreover from the bounds in Theorem 4.12
(cf. Lemma 4.13) we know that Mj → P0 in C 3,1/2 . Now, we define a sequence of blow-up maps
Njb : B1n (0) → AQ (Rn+k ) by

                                        Njb (x) := ∥Nj ∥−1
                                                        L2 (B1 (0))
                                                                    · Nj (expj (x))

where expj : B1n (0) → Mj is the exponential map at 0 ∈ Mj (here we have identified the domain
B1n (0) with the unit ball in T0 Mj ∼= Rn ). We also know from the estimates in Theorem 4.12 that
expj converges in C  2,1/2 to the identity map B1n (0) → B1n (0).

From the center manifold reverse Poincaré inequality (Corollary 4.34) we know that

                                                    ∥Njb ∥W 1,2 (B1 ) ≤ C

i.e. Njb is uniformly bounded in W 1,2 . In particular, up to passing to a subsequence, we know
that Njb converge to a W 1,2 function N b : B1n (0) → AQ (Rk ), where the convergence is strongly
                                   1,2
in L2loc (B1n (0)) and weakly in Wloc  (B1n (0))22. Notice that the limit N b is valued a.e. in AQ (Rk ),
                        ⊥
i.e. taking values in P0 rather than just Rn+k . This is readily seen from Theorem 4.12 and Lemma
4.13, since T0 M = P0 and thus Mj → P0 in C 3 (as a graph) in B1n+k (0), meaning that the normal
bundle to Mj converges to uniformly to {0}n × Rk in B1n+k (0).

Remark 4.36. In particular, from the approximate monotonicity of the frequency function over
M as shown in Theorem 4.30, we get ∥N b ∥L2 (Bρn (0)) ≥ C(V, V, ρ) > 0 for each ρ ∈ (0, 1) (this is just
blowing-up the equivalent bounds one gets from having a monotone frequency function equivalent
to those seen in Corollary 3.4 for planar frequency).

We now give two methods of how to conclude Theorem A, depending on whether one can upgrade
                                                                1,2
the above convergence of Njb to N b to strong convergence in Wloc   (B1n (0)) or not. We note that
strong convergence is known when V is the varifold corresponding to an area minimising current, or
an area minimising current mod p (this follows from the arguments in [DLS16b, Proof of Theorem
6.2] and [DLHMS20, Proof of Theorem 28.2] respectively).

Notice also that, even though the above computations are based at 0, they also hold at any point
x which is a limit point in B1n (0) of density Q branch points (or flat singular points) xi of planar
frequency ≥ 2 in Vji (as the corresponding computations over M also hold); this allows us to define
the frequency function Ix (ρ) at any such point x. We then define Ix (0) := limρ↓0 Ix (ρ), which
is well-defined at such x by the above monotonicity. We can then also deduce from the above
computation of monotonicity all the usual facts regarding Ix (ρ), such as:

    (i) if I0 (ρ) is constant on an interval [a, b], then N b is homogeneous on [a, b] of degree the
        constant value of I(ρ) on [a, b];

   (ii) if N b is homogeneous of degree α, then its spine S(N b ) (i.e. the set of points under which
        N b is translation invariant) is a subspace, which moreover is given by

                                    S(N b ) = {x : Ix (0) = I0 (0)}.


Furthermore, because we are blowing-up a sequence of rescalings of a given V about the fixed point
                                                         1,2
0, it is easy to verify from the strong convergence in Wloc  (B1n (0)) as well as the approximate almost
monotonicity of I(ρ) (Theorem 4.30), in the same manner as that seen in Section 3, that

                                I0 (ρ) = I(0)     for every ρ ∈ (0, 1),

i.e. the frequency value of N b at 0 always agrees with the center manifold frequency of V at 0, and
moreover I0 (ρ) is constant in ρ. Hence, N b must be a homogeneous function of degree I(0) ≥ 2
(from Corollary 4.31). In particular, its spine S(N b ) is a subspace, and dim(S(N b )) ̸∈ {n − 1, n}
as I0 (0) ∈ [2, ∞), and so dim(S(N b )) ≤ n − 2.

Remark 4.39. The proof that the center manifold frequency value I(0) is independent of the
choice of center manifold can also be used to verify Remark 4.32 that I(0) ≥ N (0). Indeed, given a
center manifold M, express the plane P0 as a normal graph of a single-valued function ψ over M.
Then the argument leading to (4.30) now gives
                           Z             Z
                                      2
                                 |Nf | ≤       G(N, QJψK)2 ≤ Crn+2N (0)
                                 Br (0)            Br (0)

using Corollary 3.4. Now if N (0) > I(0) one performs the analogous blow-up as in Remark 4.38
to see that again N b must vanish, giving the same contradiction as before. Hence I(0) ≥ N (0).
Notice that this argument cannot be used to show the other inequality, as all the corresponding
argument would give is that if I(0) > N (0) then the blow-up off the plane must have vanishing
average-free part, which is not necessarily a contradiction as it is entirely possible that the blow-up
off the plane would coincide with its average-part (with multiplicity Q). Notice that this can only
happen when N (0) is an integer, and so if N (0) is not an integer then we do get a contradiction
and thus need to have I(0) = N (0). In fact, the argument in Remark 4.38 (showing that the
blow-up itself is independent of the choice of center manifold) also shows that whenever the coarse
blow-up has vanishing average, then the coarse blow-up off the plane agrees with the blow-up off
the center manifold. In particular, this happens at every point where the planar frequency is non-
integer, showing that no extra information at the level of tangent maps is gained at such points
from blowing-up relative to the center manifold compared to the tangent plane (cf. Proposition
3.7).

frequency along the blow-up sequence, in the following sense: suppose x ∈ {0}k × B1n (0) is such

We are now ready to conclude the proof of Theorem A by showing that dimH (BV≥2 ∩ {ΘV =
Q}) ≤ n − 2. Notice first that, as BV≥2 is a closed set, by a simple covering argument it suffices
to show that at every point X ∈ BV≥2 with ΘV (X) = Q, there is a radius ρX > 0 such that
dimH (BV≥2 ∩ {ΘV = Q} ∩ BρX (X)) ≤ n − 2.

Thus, fix a point X ∈ BV≥2 with ΘV (X) = Q: by translating and rotating, we can without loss
of generality assume that X = 0 and the (unique) tangent cone to V at X is Q|{0} × Rn |. We
may also rescale about X = 0 to assume that, on B6n+k
                                                    √ (0), all the prior results in Section 4 hold.
                                                      n
Thus, from Theorem 4.12 we may choose a center manifold M which touches V at all points in
BV≥2 ∩ B1n+k (0); we fix such a choice M from now on. At every Y ∈ BV≥2 ∩ B1n+k (0), there is then
an associated frequency value of V relative to M, which we denote by IM V (Y ).

The blow-up analysis of V relative to M above then applies at every such point Y . As such, we
may stratify BV≥2 ∩ B1n+k (0) based on the maximal spine dimension of the blow-ups we get in this
manner. So, set for ℓ ∈ {0, 1, . . . , n − 2},

 SℓM := {Y ∈ BV≥2 ∩ B1n+k (0) : dim(S(N b )) ≤ ℓ for every blow-up N b of V relative to M at Y }.

Here, we have included the superscript M to emphasise that this stratification could depend on
the choice of M. Clearly we have

                              S0M ⊂ S1M ⊂ · · · ⊂ Sn−2
                                                   M
                                                       = BV≥2 ∩ B1n+k (0)

where the last equality follows from the discussion above.
It therefore suffices to prove that dimH (SjM ) ≤ j for each j = 0, 1, . . . , n − 2. But because we
have upper semi-continuity of the frequency along the blow-up sequence, this forces points of
high frequency compared to the base point to accumulate around the spine of the blow-up, which
has dimension at most j if the base point is in Sj . Thus, we may conclude dimH (SjM ) ≤ j in
an analogous manner to that seen in Section 3 using the weak linear approximation property of
Almgren. This therefore completes the proof of Theorem A in the case where we have local energy
convergence during the center manifold blow-up procedure.

4.4.2. When energy convergence to the center manifold blow-up is not readily available. In this
situation, we instead use the theory of multi-valued gradient Young measures introduced by Hirsch–
Spolaor [HS24]. We summarise the theory, following [HS24], in Appendix B. Here, we shall use the
results in Appendix B to prove Theorem A.
As in Section 4.4.1, we still have the identities (4.26), (4.27), and (4.28). Thus, consider Ej := EN b ,
                                                                                                       j
the elementary Young measure associated to Njb . Since (Njb )j is uniformly bounded in W 1,2 (B1 ),
the (Ej )j are uniformly bounded in Y Q , and thus by Proposition B.4 we can find F ∈ Y Q and a
subsequence such that Ej ⇀ F as Q-Young measures; in particular, F ∈ grad Y Q . Dividing again
(4.26), (4.27), and (4.28) by ∥Nj ∥2L2 (B1 (0)) , Proposition B.4 combined with Remark 4.35 tells us
that F is stationary. Remark 4.36 again gives that the associated map f ∈ W 1,2 (B1 (0); AQ (Rk ))
96               BRANCH SET STRATIFICATION FOR STATIONARY VARIFOLDS

to F given by Proposition B.7 obeys f ̸≡ 0 on B1 (0) (in fact, the proof of Proposition B.7 gives
that f = N b ).

Just as in Section 4.4.1, since the blow-up was constructed by a sequence of rescalings of a given
V about a fixed point, it is easy to verify that

                                I(0, ρ) := I(0)      for every ρ ∈ (0, 1),

and thus by Proposition B.14, we see that F must be homogeneous. Proposition B.15 then guaran-
tees that the points of maximal frequency in F belong to a subspace, which as I(0, ρ) = I(0) ≥ 2,
must be a subspace of dimension ∈ {0, 1, . . . , n − 2} by Proposition B.16. But now one can con-
clude in an analogous manner to that in Section 4.4.1, namely to stratify BV≥2 ∩ B1n+k (0) by the
maximal spine dimension of the blow-up one achieves through this procedure, and using upper
semi-continuity of the frequency along the blow-up sequence to show that nearby points of good
frequency relative to the chosen base point must accumulate along the spine of the blow-up, from
which standard measure theory again allows us to control the size. This therefore completes the
proof of Theorem A in this case also, thereby also completing its proof.                       □

                  Appendix B. Multi-Valued Gradient Young Measures
Here we provide an exposition of the multi-valued gradient Young measures introduced by Hirsch–
Spolaor [HS24]. We also provide further structural information regarding homogeneous multi-valued
gradient Young measures. Much of this appendix is included within [HS24] although there are some
differences as, for instance, we avoid the need to discuss slicings of homogeneous gradient Young
measures.

The classical theory of Young measures provides a way of associating a limit to a bounded sequence
of functions (fj )j ⊂ L∞ (B1n (0); Rk ) where the limit of φ(fj ) is determined for every continuous
function φ : Rk → R. For instance, if the fj oscillate a lot, the limiting Young measure represents
the asymptotic statistical distribution of the values of the fj near each point.

For us, we are interested in limits of sequences (fj )j of W 1,2 (B1n (0); AQ (Rk )) functions as (Young)
measures which keep track of the action of certain functions of the form φ(x, fj , Dfj , Dfj ⊗ Dfj ),
in particular those determining the inner and outer variations needed to prove the monotonicity of
a frequency function (keeping track of every such continuous φ is not possible now as we only have
W 1,2 control on the sequence). The aim is that whilst the usual weak W 1,2 limit may or may not
have a monotone frequency function, the associated Young measure (which is related to the weak
W 1,2 limit but contains more information) will have a notion of frequency which is monotone that
we can make use of.

To this end, we introduce the vector space
                                                          2
                             V := Rk × Rk×n × R(k×n) ≡ V1 × V2 × V3 .

Write (y, p, M ) ∈ V for the corresponding coordinates on V adapted to V1 , V2 , V3 . One should
think of y, p, M as playing the roles of f (x), Df (x), Df (x) ⊗ Df (x), respectively, for a function
f : B1n (0) → Rk .

Let us begin by introducing a class of test functions which W 1,2 (B1n (0); AQ (Rk )) naturally acts on.
Write Ce for the set of continuous functions φ : B1n (0) × V → R which obey:

     • spt(φ) ⊂ K × V for some compact set K ⊂ B1n (0);

     • ∥φ∥∗ < ∞, where
                                                       |φ(x, y, p, M )|
                                ∥φ∥∗ := sup                2∗      2
                                                                         .
                                            x,y,p,M 1 + |y| + |p| + |M |


Here, for n > 2 we take 2∗ := n−2
                                2n
                                   to be the Sobolev conjugate of 2, and if n = 2 we can take 2∗ to
be any (fixed) number we wish in (2, ∞), e.g. 2∗ = 3. The important properties that we will need
2∗ to satisfy is that 2∗ > 2 and we have the compact Sobolev embedding W 1,2 (B1n (0); AQ (Rk )) ,→
  ∗
L2 (B1n (0)) (which in our context follows from, e.g. [DLS11, Proposition 2.11]).

The reader should note that (C,  e ∥ · ∥∗ ) is a non-separable normed vector space (due to the non-
compactness of the support in the V-variables). Nonetheless, a function f ∈ W 1,2 (B1n (0); AQ (Rk ))
naturally gives rise to a linear map Ef : Ce → R, called the elementary Young measure associated to
f , by

                                                                  ∗

Thus, (fj )j ⊂ W 1,2 (B1n (0); AQ (Rk )) is a uniformly bounded sequence if and only if (Efj )j is uni-
formly bounded. However, since (C,  e ∥·∥∗ ) is non-separable, we cannot apply the sequential Banach–
Alaoglu theorem in this space to always find a subsequential limit of a uniformly bounded sequence.
In order to resolve this issue, we will restrict the functions in Ce we act on so that the vector space
of functions we act on is separable. Indeed, ultimately we are most interested in the limits coming
from the inner and outer variations, which correspond to the choices

                      φ1 (x, y, p, M ) := pκi y κ ∂i φ + Miiκκ φ         for φ ∈ Cc∞ (B1n (0)),

We start by proving the weak compactness of Y Q .

Proposition B.4 ([HS24, Proposition 2.2]). Suppose (Fj )j ⊂ Y Q is a sequence with supj ∥Fj ∥Y Q <
∞. Then, there exists F ∈ Y Q and a subsequence (j ′ ) such that:
      (i) ∥F∥Y Q ≤ lim inf j ∥Fj ∥Y Q ;
      (ii) Fj ′ ⇀ F.


Proof. Write Fj = (Fj ⊗ dx, Fj∞ ) for each j. Separable Banach–Alaoglu provides the existence of
some weak limit F satisfying the conclusions of the proposition, but we need to verify that F ∈ Y Q ,
i.e. that F = (F ⊗ dx, F ∞ ) for appropriate F and F ∞ .

linear maps Cc0 (B1n (0) × ∂B1V3 (0)) → R. We may therefore apply separable Banach–Alaoglu to
both giving, up to passing to a subsequence,

Cc0 (B1n (0) × ∂B1V3 (0))∗ ; once we have shown this the proof is complete, since F = (F ⊗ dx, F ∞ )

                          |(Fj ⊗ dx)(φ) − (F ⊗ dx)(χδ φ)| ≤ δ + δ sup ∥Fi ∥Y Q .
