Taking j → ∞ and then δ ↓ 0 (using dominated convergence) we therefore see that

(B.2)                                    lim (Fj ⊗ dx)(φ) = (F ⊗ dx)(φ)
                                        j→∞
104               BRANCH SET STRATIFICATION FOR STATIONARY VARIFOLDS

for any φ ∈ C(B1n (0) × V) whose recession function vanishes; in particular, the action of F ⊗ dx
on such functions is well-defined (and agrees with F̂ ). This in particular implies that M(Fx ) =
Q for a.e. x ∈ B1n (0) (the upper bound of Q comes form mass lower semi-continuity along the
weakly convergent subsequence, and the lower bound comes from taking φ = ψ(x)1V in the above
convergence for any ψ ∈ Cc∞ (B1n (0))).

Finally, we are left with a general φ ∈ C(B1n (0) × V). First notice that φ − φ∞ ∈ C(B1n (0) × V) has
vanishing recession function, and so by (B.2)

                                 (Fj ⊗ dx)(φ − φ∞ ) → (F ⊗ dx)(φ − φ∞ ).

We are therefore left with evaluating limj→∞ (Fj ⊗ dx)(φ∞ ): if we can show that this is equal to
(F ⊗ dx)(φ∞ ) + Fe∞ (φ∞ ) for a suitable linear map Fe∞ : Cc0 (B1n (0) × ∂B1V3 (0)) → R, we will be
done.

Definition B.6. We say F ∈ Y Q is a gradient Q-Young measure, and write F ∈ grad Y Q , if there

regard F ∞ as a positive Radon measure (and so ∥F ∞ ∥({|x| = r}) = 0 for a.e. r ∈ (0, 1)). Thus, by
standard approximation arguments and the Dominated Convergence Theorem, D(r) is absolutely
continuous on (0, 1), and furthermore equations such as (B.9) and the formula for D′ (r) below both
hold true for a.e. r ∈ (0, 1).

In particular, Proposition B.9 tells us that about any point x0 ∈ B1n (0) for which f ̸≡ 0 about x0 ,
we have that the frequency of f at x0 , namely

is well-defined as a number in [0, ∞).

Proposition B.10 ([HS24, Proposition 3.7(5)]). Let F be as above. Then for a given x0 ∈ B1n (0),
we either have that f ≡ 0 on B1−|x0 | (x0 ), or H(x0 , r) > 0 for all r ∈ (0, 1 − |x0 |).

Remark B.11. An immediate consequence of Proposition B.10 is that either f ≡ 0, or I(x0 , r) is
well-defined for all x0 ∈ B1n (0) and r ∈ (0, 1 − |x0 |). Indeed, if H(x0 , r) = 0 for some x0 ∈ B1n (0)
and r ∈ (0, 1 − |x0 |), then Proposition B.10 gives that f ≡ 0 on B1−|x     n     (x0 ). But then for all
                                                                               0|

y ∈ B1−|x0 | (x0 ) and sufficiently small r we have H(y, r) = 0, and so again f ≡ 0 on B1−|y|  (y) for all
y ∈ B1−|x0 | (x0 ). Repeating this eventually we get that we can take y = 0, and so f ≡ 0 on B1n (0).
      n


In particular, since we assumed that f ̸≡ 0 on B1n (0), we see that H(x0 , r) > 0 for all x0 ∈ B1n (0)
and r ∈ (0, 1 − |x0 |), meaning that I(x0 , r) is well-defined always.

Proof. Again by translating and rescaling we can without loss of generality assume that x0 = 0.
If H(r0 ) > 0, then H(r) > 0 for all r ∈ (r0 − ε, r0 + ε) for some ε > 0; let us therefore suppose (a, b)
is the largest interval containing r0 on which H > 0 (we will show that in fact (a, b) = (0, 1)). We
can then compute for r ∈ (a, b):

In particular, since H is increasing (from (B.11)), as H(r0 ) > 0 we know H > 0 on (a, 1), i.e. b = 1.

This provides a constant lower bound on H(r), and thus we see that we must in fact have H(r) > 0
for all r ∈ (0, 1), i.e. a = 0. This completes the proof.                                     □

Proposition B.12. Let F be as above. Then, the function x 7→ I(x) is upper semi-continuous.

Proof. Suppose we have xi → x ∈ B1n (0) with I(xi ) defined for all i; in particular, I(x) must be
well-defined, else f ≡ 0 on a neighbourhood of x which implies that f ≡ 0 on a neighbourhood of
xi for all i sufficiently large, a contradiction. Now fix r > 0. We clearly have D(xi , r) → D(x, r)
and H(xi , r) → H(x, r), and so I(xi , r) → I(x, r). Hence from the monotonicity of I,

                               lim sup I(xi ) ≤ lim sup I(xi , r) = I(x, r).
                                i→∞              i→∞
114              BRANCH SET STRATIFICATION FOR STATIONARY VARIFOLDS

Taking r ↓ 0 then gives the result.                                                                 □

From the proof of Proposition B.9, we get a characterisation of constant frequency via homogeneity,
i.e.:

Definition B.13. For I ∈ [0, ∞), we say that F ∈ grad Y Q is I-homogeneous if:
                   

       R

      • F ∞ (xT M κκ x) = 0.
Here, f is as in Proposition B.7 for F.

and only if F is I-homogeneous.

It is only at this point, in our final two propositions, that we deviate from [HS24]. Nonetheless, we
use arguments similar to those seen in [HS24, Proposition 3.7 & Lemma 3.11].
If F as above is I-homogeneous for some I > 0, we know that the associated function f ∈
W 1,2 (B1n (0); AQ (Rk )) is I-homogeneous; in such a situation, we will abuse notation and also write
f ∈ W 1,2 (Rn ; AQ (Rk )) for its I-homogeneous extension to Rn . In particular, standard arguments
imply that its spine
                             Sf := {z ∈ Rn : f (x + z) = f (x) for a.e. x ∈ Rn }
is a subspace of Rn . In this situation, we claim:

Proposition B.15. Suppose F as above is I-homogeneous for some I > 0. Then:

                               Sf ∩ B1n (0) = {x ∈ B1n (0) : I(x) = I(0)}.

Now we wish to show that if z ∈ B1n (0) has I(z) = I(0), then z ∈ Sf . For this, we will show that
I(z, r) is constant with r (where defined), giving that we can apply Proposition B.14 to give that

To see this, fix z ∈ Sf and r ∈ (0, 1−|z|). By the homogeneity of f based at 0 and (B.10) (or rather,
the analogous formula centered at an arbitrary point rather than 0) one can easily check that for

for any such z, r, t. But then by the monotonicity formula

                                       I(z, r) = lim I(tz, tr) ≤ lim I(tz, r).

                               I(z, r) ≤ lim I(tz, r) = I(0, r) = I,
                                                  t→0
where the last equality is from the I-homogeneity. But then since I(z, r) is increasing and
limr→0 I(z, r) = I by assumption this means that I(z, r) is constant for r ∈ (0, 1 − |z|), which
is what we needed to prove in order to complete the proof.                                    □

Proposition B.16. Suppose F as above is I-homogeneous for some I > 0 with f ̸≡ 0. Then
dim Sf ∈ {0, 1, . . . , n − 1}. Moreover, dim Sf = n − 1 only if I = 1.



Proof. Since Sf is a subspace of Rn we know that dim Sf ∈ {0, 1, . . . , n}. However, if dim Sf = n,
then f is constant almost everywhere, which being homogeneous of degree I > 0 would mean that
f ≡ 0, contradicting our assumption that f ̸≡ 0. Thus, dim Sf ∈ {0, 1, . . . , n − 1}.

Now suppose dim Sf = n − 1: we want to show that I = 1. Without loss of generality choose
coordinates of Rn so that Sf = {0} × Rn−1 , and thus f (x) ≡ f (x1 ). By Proposition B.15, we know
that I(z, r) ≡ I for all z ∈ Sf . As Sf is a subspace, this means that for any t ∈ R and i = 2, . . . , n,
if ei denotes the ith standard basis vector of Rn , then for any ψ ∈ Cc0 (B1n (0)):

zero (cf. Proposition B.14). Differentiating the above expression twice in t and using that f is

By the above, we know that G (div(φ)) = 0 for all φ ∈ Cc1 (B1n (0); Rn ), i.e. the distributional de-

But (B.14) implies, by the definition of D(r), that D(r) = Dr2 for some D ≥ 0. Then recalling
(B.11), we know that H′ (r) = 2r D(r) = 2Dr and so since limr→0 H(r) = 0 (by Proposition B.10) we
therefore get that H(r) = Dr2 (in particular, D > 0 as H(r) > 0). But then H(r) = D(r) giving
that I(r) = 1 for all r > 0, meaning that I = limr→0 I(r) = 1, as claimed.                      □
